Abstract
This thesis consists of an introduction and nine chapters, each devoted to a different combinatorial problem. What gives these problems coherence is that although at first sight they look very different, the essential difficulties in most are geometric.
In Chapters 2 and 3, we investigate sumset inequalities. For sets A, B in an abelian group G, define the sumset A + B = {a + b : a  A, b  B}. We consider the ambient groups Zk and Rk, endowed with the measure | · | representing cardinality and outer Lebesgue measure, respectively. One of the central questions in additive combinatorics is the inverse sumset problem of characterizing the finite subsets A with small doubling constant |A + A| · |A|-1. Sets A in Rk have doubling constant at least 2k; this is no longer true for sets A in Zk, unless some non-degeneracy condition is imposed. Our main result describes the structure of non-degenerate sets A in Zk with doubling constant close to 2k.
We prove a sharp stability result for the classical Freiman-Bilu 2k-inequality (improved by Green-Tao) and a generalization of Freiman's 3k - 4 theorem to arbitrary dimension. For  > 0 sufficiently small and A  Zk with |A+A|  (2k + )|A|, we show either A is covered by mk( ) parallel hyperplanes, or it satisfies |co(A) \ A|  ck |A|, where co(A) is the smallest convex progression (convex set intersected with an affine sub-lattice) containing A.
In particular, we deduce the sharp stability result for the Brunn-Minkowski inequality for equal sets, conjectured by Figalli and Jerison. Given  > 0 sufficiently small and A  Rk with |A + A|  (2k +  )|A|, we show | co(A) \ A|  ck |A|, where co(A) is the smallest convex set containing A.
Finally, we prove a strengthen version of the aforementioned conjecture by Figalli and Jerison for a specific class of geometric objects. We find the optimal constants ck, when A  Rk is the hypograph of a function defined on a convex domain in dimension k - 1  3.
In Chapters 4, 5 and 6, we investigate covering systems. A covering system is a finite collection of arithmetic progressions {a1 (mod m1), a2 (mod m2), . . . , ak (mod mk)} that cover the integers, i.e., i{ai + nmi : n  Z} = Z. Since their introduction by Erdos in 1950, covering systems have been extensively studied, and numerous questions and conjectures have been posed regarding the existence of covering systems with various properties. More than fifty years ago, Erdos asked if the moduli can be distinct and all arbitrarily large, Erdos and Selfridge asked if the moduli can be distinct and all odd, and Schinzel conjectured that in any covering system there exists a pair of moduli, one of which divides the other.
Another beautiful conjecture, proposed by Erdos and Graham in 1980, states that if the moduli are distinct elements of the interval [n,Cn], and n is sufficiently large, then the density of integers

x

uncovered by the union is bounded below by a constant (depending only on C). This conjecture

was confirmed (in a strong form) by Filaseta, Ford, Konyagin, Pomerance and Yu in 2007, who

moreover asked whether the same conclusion holds if the moduli are distinct and sufficiently large,

and

i=1 k

1 di

< C.

Although,

out,

this

condition

is

not

sufficiently

strong

to

imply

the

desired

conclusion, as one of the main results of this paper we give an essentially best possible condition

which is sufficient. More precisely, we show that if all of the moduli are sufficiently large, then the union misses a set of density at least e-4C/2, where

k C =  µ(di)
i=1 di
and µ is a multiplicative function defined by µ(pi) = 1 + (log p)3+ /p for some  > 0. We also show that no such lower bound (i.e., depending only on C) on the density of the uncovered set holds, when µ(pi) is replaced by any function of the form 1 + O(1/p).
Our method has a number of further applications. Most importantly, we prove the conjecture of Schinzel. In addition, we give an alternative (somewhat simpler) proof of a breakthrough result of Hough, who resolved Erdos' minimum modulus problem, with an improved bound on the smallest difference. Moreover, we make further progress on the problem of Erdos and Selfridge, which, in particular, we solve in the square free case.
Finally, we answer another natural question of Erdos, asked in 1952, on the number of minimal covering systems. Addressing this question requires very different methods. More precisely, we show that the number of minimal covering systems with exactly n elements is
4 n3/2 exp 3 + o(1) (log n)1/2

En route to this counting result, we obtain a structural description of all covering systems that are

close to optimal in an appropriate sense.

Chapter 7 is devoted to Littlewood polynomials. Answering a question of Erdos from 1957

and confirming a conjecture of Littlewood from 1966, we show that there exist absolute constants

 >  > 0 such that, for all n  2, there exists a polynomial P of degree n, with coefficients in {-1, 1},

such that





 n  |P(z)|   n

for all z  C with |z| = 1. Over time, this problem attracted considerable attention and, in particular, Littlewood included it in his well-known monograph containing 30 of his favourite problems.

xi

Chapter 8 is devoted to judicious partitions. Bollobás, Reed and Thomason proved that every

3-uniform hypergraph with m hyperedges has a vertex-partition into 3 parts such that each part meets

at least 13 (1 - 1e )m hyperedges. Halsegrave optimized the value to 0.6m, confirming a special case of

a conjecture of Bollobás and Thomason from 1993. For large values of m, Ma and Yu improved the

bound

asymptotically

to

0.65m + o(m).

We

further

improve

this

asymptotic

bound

to

19 27

m

+

o(m),

which is best possible up to the error term, resolving the next open case of a conjecture of Bollobás

and Scott from 2000.

Chapter 9 is devoted to coloured structures in the Boolean lattice. Given a collection of coloured

chain posets, we estimate the number of coloured subsets of the Boolean lattice which avoid all

chains in this collection. Our proof relies on the recent powerful method of hypergraph containers

developed independently by Balogh, Morris and Samotij as well as Saxton and Thomason, inspired

by the previous work of Conlon and Gowers. In order to prove results about coloured chain posets we

need to apply the hypergraph container lemma recursively, with different uniformities at each stage,

using a balanced supersaturation result for a certain non-uniform hypergraph encoding forbidden

configurations. Our work extends to a coloured setting the previous work of Collares and Morris

as well as Balogh, Mycroft, and Treglown on the number of antichains in a random subset of the

Boolean lattice.

Chapter 10 is devoted to positional games. For two graphs B and H the strong Ramsey game

R(B, H) on the board B and with target H is played as follows. Two players alternately claim edges of

B. The first player to build a copy of H wins. If none of the players win, the game is declared a draw.

A notorious open question of Beck asks whether the first player has a winning strategy in R(Kn, Kk)

in bounded time as n  . Surprisingly, in a recent paper Hefetz, Kusch, Narins, Pokrovskiy, Requilé

and Sarid constructed a 5-uniform hypergraph H for which they proved that the first player does not have a winning strategy in R(Kn(5), H ) in bounded time. They naturally asked whether an analogous

result holds for graphs. We make further progress towards this question.

Chapter 1
Introduction

This thesis is organised into ten chapters of which this introduction is the first. Chapters 2 and 3 investigate the stability of sumset inequalities. Chapters 4, 5 and 6 investigate covering systems. Chapter 7 is devoted to Littlewood polynomials. Chapter 8 is devoted to judicious partitions. Chapter 9 is devoted to coloured structures in the Boolean lattice. Chapter 10 is devoted to positional games.

1.1 Stability of sumset inequalities
The results in Chapters 2 and 3 are all joint with Peter van Hintum and Hunter Spink [95-98]. The fundamental notion that we explore here is that of sumset. Given two subsets A and B of an
abelian group G, we define their sumset
A + B = {a + b : a  A, b  B}.
The ambient groups G that we consider are Zk and Rk. In the former case | · | refers to cardinality and in the latter case | · | refers to the outer Lebesgue measure.

1.1.1 Discrete Setting

In the discrete setting, the most basic sumset inequality says that if A and B are non-empty finite

subsets of Z then

|A + B|  |A| + |B| - 1

and equality holds if and only if A and B are arithmetic progressions with the same common difference or one of them is a singleton. Cauchy [41] was the first to study sumsets in Zp rather than Z, establishing a corresponding statement there. Over one hundred years later, his result was rediscovered by Davenport [48, 49] and was further generalized by Kneser [106] to abelian groups.
One of the central questions in additive combinatorics is the inverse sumset problem of characterizing the finite subsets A of abelian groups with small doubling constant |A + A| · |A|-1.

2

Introduction

We consider the inverse sumset problem in torsion-free abelian groups Zk, which has been studied from a variety of perspectives by Freiman [79, 80], Green and Tao [85], Chang [42], and Sanders [133] among others. However, we remark that the setting of arbitrary abelian groups has been studied by Green and Ruzsa [83]; the setting of non-abelian groups represents the scope of the recent literature on approximate groups which culminates with the work of Breuillard, Green and Tao [34].
In his seminal work [79], Freiman gave the first qualitative answer to the inverse sumset problem in Zk. There are two closely related formulations of Freiman's theorem of which one relies on the notion of generalized arithmetic progressions and the other relies on the notion of convex progressions.
Definition 1.1.1. An r-dimensional generalized arithmetic progression B in Zk is a subset of the form B = b +  (Zr  K), where b  Zk, K is a axis-aligned box in Rr and  : Zr  Zk is a homomorphism. Equivalently, an r-dimensional generalized arithmetic progression B in Zk is a subset of the form
r

i=1
where n1, . . . , nr  N and b, v1, . . . , vr  Zk. More generally, an r-dimensional convex progression P in Zk is a subset of the form P =
p +  (Zr  K), where p  Zk, K is a convex1 body in Rr and  : Zr  Zk is a homomorphism.
We also define a notion of degeneracy, which turns out to be central in our results.
Definition 1.1.2. We call B a non-degenerate r-dimensional generalized arithmetic progression and P a non-degenerate r-dimensional convex progression in Zk if additionally  : Zr  Zk is an injective homomorphism.
Equivalently, a non-degenerate r-dimensional generalized arithmetic progression B in Zk is a subset of the form B(n1, . . . , nr; v1, . . . , vr; b) := {b + i=1 r ivi : 0  i < ni} , where n1, . . . , nr  N and b, v1, . . . , vr  Zk and v1, . . . , vr are linearly independent.
A non-degenerate r-dimensional convex progression P in Zk is a subset of the form P =   K where  is an r-dimensional affine sub-lattice2 in Zk and K is a convex body in Rk.
We digress to justify the equivalence above. Say P = p +  (Zr  K), where p  Zk, K is a convex body in Rr and  : Zr  Zk is an injective homomorphism. We can extend  to an injective linear map  : Rr  Rk. Then P = p +  (Zr  K) = (p +  (Zr))  (p +  (K)), where p +  (Zr) is an affine sub-lattice and p +  (K) is convex. Conversely, say P =   K where  is an r-dimensional affine sub-lattice and K is convex. By the fundamental theorem of finitely generated abelian groups, it follows that  = p +  (Zr), where  : Zr  Zk is an injective homomorphism. We can extend  to an injective linear map  : Rr  Rk. We conclude P =   K = p + (-p + )  (-p + K) = p +  (Zr)  (-p + K) = p +  (Zr   -1(-p + K)), and  -1(-p + K) is a convex set in Rr.

1.1 Stability of sumset inequalities

3

Note that any k-dimensional convex progression in Zk that is not contained in a single hyperplane is non-degenerate. Conversely, any non-degenerate convex progression in Zk is (at most)
k-dimensional (and we can always view it as being exactly k-dimensional).

Freiman's theorem states that for any  > 0 there exist b1( ), b2( ), b3( ) > 0 such that the following holds. Any finite subset A  Zk with doubling constant less than  can be covered by b1( ) translates of a generalized arithmetic progression (or alternatively convex progression) of size at most
b2( )|A|, and of dimension at most b3( ).

Freiman introduced the notion of convex progression in his original work and much of the literature builds on this notion. In particular, generalizations of convex progressions were used implicitly in results of Bourgain [31] and Green and Sanders [84] (see Sander's extensive survey [133] for more information). We note that many authors require convex progressions to be symmetric, but here we impose no such assumptions.
Motivated by Freiman's theorem, much of the study of inverse sumset problems has focused on the following quantitative question.
Question 1.1.3. In Freiman's theorem, what are the optimal parameters b1( ), b2( ), b3( )?
Of course, the optimal parameters should vary, depending on how we prioritize them. Moreover, they might vary between the two formulations of Freiman's theorem, i.e., using generalized arithmetic progressions or using convex progressions.

Firstly, say we want to minimize the maximum of b1( ), b2( ), exp(b3( )). In Freiman's formulation using generalized arithmetic progressions, Lovett and Regev [118] showed that the parameters b1( ), b2( ), and exp(b3( )) cannot all be brought down to polynomial. However, in Freiman's formulation using (symmetric) convex progressions, Freiman and Ruzsa [131] conjectured that such a polynomial bound exists. In spite of several serious attempts, the polynomial Freiman-Ruzsa conjecture remains open. Green and Ruzsa [83], Chang [42], Bourgain [31] and Green and Tao [86] showed the parameters could be reduced to b1( ) = b2( ) = exp(b3( )) = exp(O(C)) for some constant C. This was improved by Schoen [137] to exp(exp(O( log( ))), and finally improved by Sanders [133] to exp(O(log3+o(1)  )).

Secondly, say we want to reduce the dimension b3( ) and the size b2( ) at the cost of the number
of translates b1( ). In Freiman's formulation using generalized arithmetic progressions, Green and Tao [85] showed that for   [2r, 2r+1 -  ) we may take b2( ) = 1 and b3( ) = log2( ), which are optimal, and b1( ) = exp( O (1)). This builds on the earlier work of Freiman and Bilu [23, 80].
The Green-Tao improvement of Freiman-Bilu 2k-inequality essentially states that for any  > 0, there is a constant mk( ) such that given A  Zk, either |A + A|  (2k -  )|A| or A is covered by mk( ) parallel hyperplanes.

4

Introduction

This suggests that the correct notion of degeneracy for a set A in Zk is being covered by a bounded number of parallel hyperplanes, and non-degenerate sets A have doubling constant bounded below by roughly 2k. This reflects the continuous analogue for A  Rk. There is a large literature of classifications of subsets A  Zk with doubling at most 2k -  (see, e.g., Fishburn [77], Freiman [80], Grynkiewicz and Serra [87], and Stanchescu [144-147]). However, little is known beyond the 2k
threshold.

Finally, say we want to reduce the dimension b3( ) and the number of translates b1( ) at the cost of the size b2( ). Observe that we always have b1( )  1 and b3( )  log2( ). Indeed, fix r  N and choose n  N large. Note that the set [n]r has doubling constant less than 2r. Also, [n]r is not covered by a bounded number of parallel hyperplanes and thus by a bounded number of translates of a generalized arithmetic progressions of dimension r - 1. Restricting the scope to non-degenerate sets A  Zk with doubling   2k, gives rise to the following qualitative and quantitative questions.
Question 1.1.4. In Freiman's theorem, is it possible to take b1( ) = 1 and b3( ) = log2( )? If so what is the optimal value of b2( )?
Marking the beginning of the study of inverse problems in additive combinatorics, the classical Freiman 3|A| - 4 theorem [80] answers Question 1.1.4 in Z, opening the path to Zk.
Freiman's 3|A| - 4 theorem states that if A is a subset of the integers with |A + A|  3|A| - 4, then A is contained in an arithmetic progression P with |P \ A|  |A + A| - 2|A| + 1.
Results
Our main result in the discrete setting [96] gives a complete answer to Question 1.1.4 in Zk, i.e., we establish a sharp stability result for Freiman-Bilu 2k-inequality (improved by Green and Tao [85]) and we obtain a natural generalization of Freiman's 3|A| - 4 theorem in arbitrary dimension.

The exponents ak, bk obtained in [69] are double exponential in the dimension k. This naturally gives rise to the following question proposed by Figalli and Jerison, which attracted considerable attention lately.
Question 1.1.9. What are the optimal exponents ak, bk, prioritized in this order?
The exponents ak, bk are prioritized in this order because if the inequality holds for (ak, bk), then the inequality also holds for (ak, bk) whenever ak > ak by taking dk () sufficiently small.
Before their general result [69] for arbitrary sets A, B, Figalli and Jerison [67] had considered the case of equal sets A = B and gave a polynomial upper bound   Ck ak . 1.2 Covering systems

The results in Chapters 4, 5 and 6 are all joint with Paul Balister, Béla Bollobás, Robert Morris and

Julian Sahasrabudhe [6, 3, 5].

The fundamental notion explored here is that of covering systems. A covering system is a finite

collection A1, . . . , Ak of arithmetic progressions that cover the integers, i.e., that satisfy

k i=1

Ai

=

Z.

Throughout the thesis we exclude the trivial arithmetic progression Z.

1.2.1 Density

The study of covering systems with distinct differences (or moduli) 1 < d1 < · · · < dk was initiated in 1950 by Erdos [55], who used them to answer a question of Romanoff. Since their introduction, covering systems have been extensively studied, and numerous questions and conjectures have been posed regarding the existence of covering systems with various properties.
For example, Erdos [55] asked whether there exist such systems with minimum modulus arbitrarily large, Erdos and Selfridge (see, e.g., [75]) asked if there exists a covering system with all moduli distinct and odd, and Schinzel [136] conjectured that in any covering system there exist a pair of moduli, one of which divides the other. In 1980, Erdos and Graham [60] initiated the study of the density of the uncovered set; in particular, they conjectured that if the (distinct) moduli d1, . . . , dk all lie in the interval [n,Cn], where n  n0(C) is sufficiently large, then the uncovered set has density at least  for some  = (C) > 0.
The first significant progress on these problems was made by Filaseta, Ford, Konyagin, Pomerance and Yu [76] in 2007, who proved (in a strong form) the conjecture of Erdos and Graham, and took an important step towards solving Erdos' minimum modulus problem. Moreover, Filaseta, Ford, Konyagin, Pomerance and Yu proposed the following natural and beautiful question.

Question 1.2.1. Is it true that for each C > 0, there exist constants M > 0 and  > 0 such that the following holds: for every covering system whose distinct moduli satisfy

k1
d1, . . . , dk  M and  < C,
i=1 di

(1.1)

the uncovered set has density at least ?

Building on their work, and in a remarkable breakthrough, Hough [100] resolved the minimum modulus problem in 2015, showing that in every covering system with distinct moduli, the minimum modulus is at most 1016. The method of [100] was further refined by Hough and Nielsen [101], who used it to prove that every covering system with distinct differences contains a difference that is divisible by either 2 or 3. However, Hough's method does not appear to be strong enough to resolve the problem of Erdos and Selfridge, and it moreover gives little information about the density of the uncovered set, as asked by Filaseta, Ford, Konyagin, Pomerance and Yu.

1.2 Covering systems

11

Results

The purpose of Chapter 4 and 5 is to develop a general method, for bounding the density of the set left uncovered by a collection of arithmetic progressions. The work in these chapters is joint with Paul Balister, Béla Bollobás, Robert Morris and Julian Sahasrabudhe [6, 5]. [6] has been published in Inventiones mathematicae and [5] has been published in Algebra and Number Theory. Our central result gives a complete answer to Question 1.2.1 of Filaseta, Ford, Konyagin, Pomerance and Yu [76]. Our method, which is based on that of Hough [100], but is actually somewhat simpler, turns out to be sufficiently powerful and flexible to allow us to also make further progress on the problem of Erdos and Selfridge [75], and to prove Schinzel's conjecture [136].
We answer Question 1.2.1 negatively for every C  1, by constructing (a sequence of) families of arithmetic progressions with arbitrarily large moduli, for which the density of the set missed by these arithmetic progressions is arbitrarily small, and sum of the reciprocals of the moduli is less than 1. However, we answer a variation of Question 1.2.1 positively by showing that for a collection of arithmetic progressions with distinct and sufficiently large moduli, the density of the set missed by these arithmetic progressions is controlled by a weighted sum of the reciprocals of the moduli.

It would be extremely interesting to characterize the functions µ such that, under the conditions of Theorem 1.2.2, the density of the uncovered set is bounded from below by a constant  (C) > 0 depending only on C.
Note that Hough's theorem [100] is an immediate consequence of Theorem 1.2.2. Our method of sieving is inspired by that of Hough [100], but is simpler in various important ways (for example, we do not need to appeal to the Lovász Local Lemma, and do not need his notion of quasi-randomness), and as a result we obtain a somewhat simpler proof of his theorem, with a better bound on the minimum difference.

Theorem 1.2.5. Let A be a finite collection of arithmetic progressions with distinct moduli d1, . . . , dk  616000. Then A does not cover the integers.

Although our sieve is developed to control the density of the uncovered set, it turns out that it can be used to prove a number of additional interesting results about covering systems. We focus here on the two classical examples mentioned above: the question of Erdos and Selfridge, and the conjecture of Schinzel.
Over 50 years ago, Erdos and Selfridge (see [75] or [136]) asked whether or not there exist covering systems with distinct odd moduli.6 This question appears to have first been asked by Erdos [57] in 1965, and a few years later he conjectured (see [58]) that there does exist such a system. In 1977 he went further, conjecturing [59] that there exist covering systems with square-free moduli, all of whose prime factors are arbitrarily large. On the other hand (as recounted, for example, in [75]), Selfridge believed that there do not exist such systems, and (perhaps as a result) the question has become known as the Erdos-Selfridge problem. Apart from its intrinsic appeal, the problem is motivated by a theorem of Schinzel [136], who discovered a connection between the non-existence of such covering systems and the irreducibility of certain polynomials. More precisely, he showed that if no covering system with distinct, odd moduli exists, then for every polynomial f (x)  Z[X] with
6Moreover, as recounted in [75], Erdos (who thought that such coverings are likely to exist) offered $25 for a proof that there is no covering with these properties, and Selfridge (who expected the opposite) offered $300 (later increased to $2000) for a construction of such a covering.

1.2 Covering systems

13

f  1, f (0) = 0 and f (1) = -1, there exists an infinite arithmetic progression of values of n  Z such that xn + f (x) is irreducible over the rationals. He also showed that this would imply the following statement: in any covering system, one of the moduli divides another. We prove this latter statement, known as Schinzel's conjecture.
Theorem 1.2.6. If A is a finite collection of arithmetic progressions that covers the integers, then at least one of the moduli divides another.
Unfortunately, our method does not seem to be strong enough to resolve the Erdos-Selfridge problem (see the discussion in Section 4.6). However, it does allow us to make some further progress towards a solution; in particular, we can prove that no such covering system exists under the additional constraint that the moduli are square-free. This application of our sieve requires several additional (somewhat technical) ideas.
Theorem 1.2.7. If A is a finite collection of arithmetic progressions with distinct square-free moduli that covers the integers, then at least one of the moduli is even.
A different strengthening of the condition in the Erdos-Selfridge problem was considered recently by Hough and Nielsen [101], who showed that in any covering system with distinct moduli, one of the moduli is divisible by either 2 or 3. Their proof required careful optimization of their techniques, and it seems difficult to use it to strengthen their result. Using our methods, we give a short proof of the following strenthening of their theorem.
Theorem 1.2.8. Let A = {Ad : d  D} be a finite collection of arithmetic progressions with distinct moduli that covers the integers, and let Q = lcm(D) be the least common multiple of the moduli. Then either 2 | Q, or 9 | Q, or 15 | Q.
In other words, either there is an even d, a d divisible by 32 = 9, or there are d1, d2  D (possibly equal) with 3 | d1 and 5 | d2. We remark that we are unable to prove that a single d  D has 15 | d in this last case.
1.2.2 Number
We turn our attention to another problem on covering systems, whose study was initiated by Erdos [56] in 1952:
Question 1.2.9. How many minimal covering systems of size n are there?
Erdos [56] gave a simple proof that there are only finitely many minimal7 covering systems of size n, but the bound he obtained on their number was doubly exponential. A more reasonable upper bound follows from a result of Simpson [140], who proved in 1985 (see Section 6.2) that the largest
7A covering system A is minimal if no proper subset of it covers Z. Without this restriction there are infinitely many covering systems of size 2, since we can take A = {Z, A} for any arithmetic progression A.

modulus in a minimal covering system of size n is at most 2n-1. Note that this bound is best possible, since A = 2i-1 (mod 2i) : i  [n - 1]  0 (mod 2n-1) is a minimal covering system, and that it easily implies that there are at most 2O(n2) minimal covering systems of size n. As it turns out, there
are in fact fewer such systems, which naturally gives rise to the problem of determining asymptotically
the logarithm of their number.

Results
In Chapter 6, we resolve Erdos' problem [56], i.e., we determine the number of minimal covering systems of a given size up to a 1 + o(1) factor in the exponent. In doing so we obtain a structural description of all covering systems that are close to optimal in an appropriate sense. The work in this chapter is joint with Paul Balister, Béla Bollobás, Robert Morris and Julian Sahasrabudhe [3].

1.3 Littlewood polynomials

1.3 Littlewood polynomials

In Chapter 7, we answer a question of Erdos from 1957 [63] and confirm a conjecture of Littlewood from 1966 [112] on the existence of flat Littlewood polynomials. The work in this chapter is joint with Paul Balister, Béla Bollobás, Robert Morris and Julian Sahasrabudhe and is published in Annals of Mathematics [4].
A Littlewood polynomial is a polynomial all of whose coefficients are 1 or -1. This notion has been extensively studied over the past century (see [123]), originating in the work of Hardy and Littlewood [91], Bloch and Pólya [24] and Littlewood and Offord [109, 115, 116]. Inspired by a list of problems posed by Erdos [63], Littlewood [110-113] wrote a series of papers on the extremal properties of polynomials with restricted coefficients, where he made the following conjecture.

Conjecture 1.3.1. There exist constants  >  > 0 such that, for all n  2, there exists a Littlewood





polynomial P(z) of degree n with  n  |P(z)|   n for all z  C with |z| = 1.

The bounds are almost as good as one can hope for, given that for a Littlewood polynomial P(z) of degree n the average value of |P(z)|2 over the complex unit circle is n + 1. Soon after its publication, the conjecture acquired great notoriety and resisted several attempts of proof e.g., [114]. This chapter settles Conjecture 1.3.1 in full generality.

16

Introduction

1.4 Judicious partitions

In Chapter 8, we resolve the next open case of a conjecture of Bollobás and Scott [28] from 2000 on judicious partitions [28]. The work in this chapter is joint with Hunter Spink and is published in Random Structures and Algorithms [142].
Numerous classical partitioning problems in combinatorics ask for a single quantity to be maximized or minimized over the set of partitions of a combinatorial object such as a hypergraph. Perhaps the best known example is the Max-Cut problem that asks for the largest bipartite subgraph of a given graph. In contrast, judicious partitioning problems ask for various quantities to be maximized or minimized simultaneously. Their study was initiated by Bollobás and Scott in 1993 [26], who posed a number of beautiful judicious partitioning problems that attracted considerable attention over time. A well known conjecture of Bollobás and Scott [28] from 2000 states the following.

Conjecture 1.4.1. Every r-uniform hypergraph with m hyperedges has a vertex-partition in k parts

such

that

each

part

meets

at

least

(1

-

(1

-

1 k

)r

)m

+

o(m)

hyperedges,

and

this

number

is

optimal.

This is intuitive because in a random partition in k parts of the vertices of a r-uniform hypergraph with m hyperedges, the expected number of hyperedges each part meets is (1 - (1 - 1k )r)m. Nevertheless, even the case r = k = 3 of this conjecture resisted several attempts of proof [25, 28, 93, 119].
Actually, the original motivation for the case r = k = 3 came from a related conjecture of Bollobás and Thomason with the quantity (1 - (1 - 13 )3)m + o(m) replaced by the quantity 3m5 . This chapter settles the next open case of Conjecture 1.4.1, i.e., the case r = k = 3.

1.5 Coloured structures in the Boolean lattice

17

1.5 Coloured structures in the Boolean lattice
In Chapter 9, we determine the number of coloured subsets of the Boolean lattice avoiding certain coloured chains patterns up to a 1 + o(1) factor in the exponent. The work in this chapter is joint with Hunter Spink and is published in the European Journal of Combinatorics [143].
Numerous well studied problems in the literature concern families of combinatorial objects with forbidden structures. The method of hypergraph containers, developed independently by Balogh, Morris and Samotij [7] and Saxton and Thomason [134], inspired by the previous work of Conlon and Gowers [46], has had a tremendous success in producing enumerative, structural and extremal results in a wide variety of settings (see [8]). For example, a classical result of Kleitman [105] estimates the number of antichains in the Boolean lattice P([n]). Using the hypergraph container algorithm, independently Collares and Morris [45] and Balogh, Mycroft, and Treglown [9] were able to count the number of antichains in a random subset of P([n]), from which they were able to deduce the approximate size of the largest antichain therein. This gives rise to the following analogous questions in a coloured setting.
Question 1.5.1. Fix colours c1, . . . , cm and fix a collection G of coloured chain posets 8. What is the number of coloured subsets X of P([n]) with the property that X does not induce coloured chains of G?
Question 1.5.2. Fix colours c1, . . . , cm, fix parameters p0, . . . , pm > 0 with p0 + . . . + pm = 1 and fix a collection G of coloured chain posets. Assume we colour each element of P([n]) independently with colour ci with probability pi and erase it with probability 1 -  pi, in order to produce a random coloured subset Y of P([n]). What is the expected number of induced coloured subsets X of Y with the property that X does not induce coloured chains of G ?
In this chapter we answer these two questions in the sparse setting, i.e., when G contains a monochromatic chain of each colour, complementing the recent result of Falgas-Ravry, O'Connell, and Uzzell [64] in the dense setting.

8Poset is the abbreviation for partial ordered set

18

Introduction

1.6 Positional games
In Chapter 10, we make further progress on a question of Beck [19] and a question of Hefetz, Kusch, Narins, Pokrovskiy, Requilé and Sarid [94] on the existence of Ramsey Games in which the first player has a winning strategy but not in bounded time. The work in this chapter is joint with Stefan David and Ivailo Hartarsky and is published in the European Journal of Combinatorics [50].
The theory of positional games on graphs and hypergraphs goes back to the seminal work of Hales and Jewett [88] and Erdos and Selfridge [62], but it was turned into an important area of combinatorics by a series of papers of Beck [13-15, 17-20]. In recent years, the theory has seen exciting developments thanks to its connections with areas such as Ramsey theory, extremal graph theory and probabilistic combinatorics. There are several interesting types of positional games but perhaps the most natural ones are the so-called strong games of which the Ramsey games are a prominent example. In the Ramsey game R(B, H) on a graph B, called the board, with target graph H, two players alternate to take previously unclaimed edges of B. The earliest player to claim an isomorphic copy of H wins and if none does so in finite time, the game is declared a draw.
A simple strategy stealing argument due to Nash combined with Ramsey's theorem shows that in R(Kn, H), where H is a fixed graph and Kn is a complete graph with n sufficiently large, the first player has winning strategy. It might be tempting to go one step further and ask if the first player has a winning strategy in bounded time as n   (see [94]). There are a few target graphs for which the answer is known to be positive and there are no target graphs for which the answer is known to be negative. In particular, even in the simple case when H = K5, the answer is not known. Beck popularized this as one of his "7 most humiliating open problems" [19]. Surprisingly, Hefetz, Kusch, Narins, Pokrovskiy, Requilé and Sarid [94] considered the analogous question for 5-uniform hypergraphs and constructed a target 5-uniform hypergraph for which they showed the answer to be negative. However, going from hypergraphs to graphs poses several difficulties.
In this chapter we consider the analogous question for 4-uniform hypergraphs, and construct a target 4-uniform hypergraph for which we show the answer to be negative. Also, we consider the analogous question for graphs where we replace the board by two disjoint copies of the board, and construct a target graph for which we show the answer to be negative.

Chapter 2
Sets in Zk with doubling 2k +  are near convex progressions
The work in this section is joint with Peter van Hintum and Hunter Spink [96].
2.1 Introduction
In this chapter we prove Theorem 1.1.5, the sharp stability result for the Freiman-Bilu 2k-inequality (improved by Green and Tao [85]) and the generalization of Freiman's 3k - 4 theorem [80] to arbitrary dimension. A corollary of this theorem is the sharp stability for the Brunn-Minkowski inequality for equal sets conjectured by Figalli and Jerison [71]. For convenience, we introduce some notations and conventions and reformulate Theorem 1.1.5 (and its corollaries) as Theorem 2.1.1 (and its corollaries). We include a brief introduction here (for a comprehensive introduction see Section 1.1).
Our starting point is Freiman's theorem which states that for any  > 0 there exist b1( ), b2( ), b3( ) > 0 such that the following holds. Any finite subset A  Zk with doubling constant less than  can be covered by b1( ) translates of a generalized arithmetic progression (or alternatively convex progression) (see Definition 1.1.1) of size at most b2( )|A|, and of dimension at most b3( ). There is a rich literature on optimizations of the parameters b1( ), b2( ), b3( ).
A notorious open problem is the polynomial Freiman-Ruzsa conjecture [131] which says that we can take b1( ), b2( ) and exp(b3( )) to be polynomial in  , which if true would be best possible. In Freiman's formulation using generalized arithmetic progressions, Lovett and Regev [118] showed that these parameters cannot be simultaneously brought down to polynomial in  . However, in Freiman's formulation using (symmetric) convex progressions, a succession of improvements by Green and Ruzsa [83], Chang [42], Bourgain [31], Green and Tao [86], Schoen [137] and Sanders [133] showed that these parameters can be simultaneously brought down to exp(O(log3+o(1)  )).
An important result of Green and Tao [85], building on a previous result of Freiman and Bilu [23, 80], says that if 2k-1    2k -  then we can take b2( ) = 1 and b3( ) = log2( ), which are essentially best possible, provided we allow b1( ) = exp( O (1)).

20

Sets in Zk with doubling 2k +  are near convex progressions

In particular, this last result says that for any  > 0 there is a constant mk( ) such that given A  Zk either |A + A|  2k -  or A is covered by mk( ) parallel hyperplanes. This suggests that the correct notion of degeneracy for sets A  Zk is being covered by a bounded number of parallel hyperplanes. Thus, non-degenerate sets A  Zk have doubling constant bounded below by roughly 2k, which mirrors the fact that sets A  Rk have doubling constant (with respect to volume) bounded below by 2k.
There is a large literature of classifications of subsets A  Zk with doubling  at most 2k -  (see, e.g., Fishburn [77], Freiman [80], Grynkiewicz and Serra [87], and Stanchescu [144-147]). However, little is known beyond the 2k threshold.
Our main result says that, restricting the scope to sets A  Zk not covered by a bounded number of parallel hyperplanes, if 2k    2k + k then we can take b1( ) = 1 and b3( ) = log2( ), which are essentially best possible, provided we allow b2( ) to be sufficiently large. Moreover, our result
says we can take b2( ) = Ok(1) in Freiman's formulation using generalized arithmetic progressions, and b2( ) = 1 + Ok(1)( - 2k) in Freiman's formulation using generalized arithmetic progressions,
which are optimal up to the constant factor Ok(1). In particular, our result says that for any  > 0 there is a constant mk( ) such that given A  Zk
either A is covered by mk( ) parallel hyperplanes or A is contained inside a convex progression C
of dimension k and size at most (1 + Ok(1) )|A|. This result can be regarded as an extension of
Freiman's 3k - 4 theorem in arbitrary dimension.
Actually, we show our result using only non-degenerate generalized arithmetic progression and
non-degenerate convex progressions. We recall that a non-degenerate r-dimensional generalized arithmetic progression in Zk is a
subset of the form B(n1, . . . , nr; v1, . . . , vr; b) := {b + i=1 r ivi : 0  i < ni}, where b, v1, . . . , vr  Zk and v1, v2, . . . , vr are independent.
We also recall that a non-degenerate r-dimensional convex progression in Zk is a subset of the form P =   K where  is an r-dimensional affine subllatice in Zk and K is a convex set in Rk.
Any non-degenerate convex progression in Zk is (at most) k-dimensional (and we can always view it as being exactly k-dimensional).
We adopt the convention that whenever we refer to generalized arithmetic progressions or to
convex progressions we mean non-degenerate ones unless otherwise explicitly stated. For A  Zk, define the convex progression hull co(A) to be the intersection of the real convex
hull co(A) with the affine sub-lattice A which is the translate of span(A - A) containing A. The
convex progression hull co(A) is the smallest convex progression containing A as any affine sub-lattice
containing A must contain A and any convex set containing A must contain co(A). In particular, A is
a convex progression if and only if A = co(A). Finally, define dk(A) = |A + A| - 2k|A|. Our main result describes the structure of A  Zk with
dk(A)  k|A|, i.e., |A + A|  (2k + k)|A|.

2.1.1 Outline of the chapter
As mentioned before, most of the work is devoted to proving Theorem 2.1.4. The strategy is to construct in stages a highly structured set A from A with the properties that |A  A| = o0(1)|A| and dk(A) = o0(1)|A| = o0(1)|A| (where for fixed 0 we have o0(1)  0 as   0). The additional structure of A enables us to conclude that |co(A) \ A| = o0(1)|A|, which finally implies that |co(A) \ A| = o0(1)|A|.
At each stage we produce a new set Anew from an existing set Aold which satisfies |A  Aold| = o0(1)|A| and dk(Aold) = o0(1)|A|. With a single exception, this is done by throwing away rows Rx in the first coordinate direction which are are in some sense unstructured. If we can show |Aold \ Anew| = o0(1)|A|, then |A  Anew| = o0(1)|A| and hence we have dk(Anew) = o0(1)|A|.
To bound |Aold \ Anew| from above, we introduce an operation (+) in order to create a "reference set" Aold(+)Aold  Aold + Aold, whose size we can guarantee to be approximately 2k|Aold|. For sets X,Y  Z we define X(+)Y := (X + minY )  (Y + max X)  X + Y . For sets X  Z × {x2} × . . . × {xk} and Y  Z × {y2} × . . . × {yk} we define X(+)Y := (X + minY )  (Y + max X)  X + Y , by using the order on X and the order on Y induced by the first coordinate. In general, we define Aold(+)Aold := v{0}×{0,1}k-1 x{0}×Zk-1 Rx(+)Rx+v  Aold + Aold. This union is disjoint1 as each y  {0} × Zk-1 can be written uniquely as y = x + (x +v). In particular, |Aold(+)Aold|  v{0}×{0,1}k-1 x{0}×Zk-1 |Rx(+)Rx+v|.
In order to control the size of the unstructured rows U := x - unstructured Rx, we construct a set D  U + Aold  Aold + Aold of comparable size to U, disjoint from Aold(+)Aold. Then, we obtain |Aold \ Anew| = |U |  |D|  |Aold + Aold| - |Aold(+)Aold|  dk(Aold) = o0 (1)|A|.
For this approach to work it is crucial that the reference set satisfies |Aold(+)Aold|  2k|Aold|, or equivalently, v{0,1}k-1 x |Rx(+)Rx+v|  2k x |Rx|. It may seem surprising that this very restricted subset could account for such a big proportion. And indeed this is not true in general. However consider the following situation. Say that Aold  [n]k has density at least 0 and that the projection of Aold in the first coordinate (Aold) is approximately a convex progression, say it satisfies |co((Aold))\ (Aold)|  o0(1)|(Aold)|. For the last step in the proof of Theorem 2.1.4 and for the proof of Theorem 2.1.1 b), we use two versions of an argument inspired by the one used in [98]. For the last part of Theorem 2.1.4, we prove that functions on convex domains with small infimum-convolution are close to their convex hulls (which is essentially Theorem 2.1.5). For Theorem 2.1.1 b), we proceed as follows. By choosing an appropriately small k(0), Theorem 2.1.4 ensures |co(A) \ A| · |A|-1 is as small as we like. This guarantees a large interior region of co(A + A) is contained in A + A. We control the size of co(A) \ A by inductively controlling the size of co(A) \ A restricted to certain homothetic copies of co(A) used to cover a thickened boundary of co(A). This allows us to show that |co(A + A) \ (A + A)|  (2k - ck)|co(A) \ A| + o0(1)|B| for some constant ck, which allows us to conclude Theorem 2.1.1 b).
Finally, for Theorem 2.1.1 a), we start with a result of Green and Tao [85] that A is covered by a bounded number of generalized arithmetic progressions of dimension k and size at most |A|, and show that we can reduce to a single generalized arithmetic progression of size O(|A|).
In Section 2, we make some initial definitions, conventions and observations, which are used throughout the chapter. In Sections 3 and 4, we prove Theorem 2.1.4 and Theorem 2.1.1 b) with a simultaneous induction on dimension. In Section 5, we prove Theorem 2.1.1 a), whose proof is independent of Sections 3 and 4. Finally, for completeness, we include in Section 2.6 a proof of Corollary 2.1.2 and Corollary 2.1.3 from Theorem 2.1.1.

2.2 Definitions, Conventions, and Observations
In this section, we introduce our definitions and conventions, as well as observations we will be using throughout the remaining sections.
2.2.1 Definitions and Conventions
Convention 2.2.1. We use the symbol to denote a disjoint union. Generalized arithmetic progressions and convex progressions are assumed to be non-degenerate ones unless otherwise stated. Definition 2.2.2. For A  Zk, we denote by
· co(A)  Rk for the convex hull, · co(A) = co(A)  Zk, · A = A - a + a  Zk for any a  A, the affine sublattice of Zk spanned by A, and · co(A) = co(A)  A, the smallest convex progression containing A. Definition 2.2.3. We say that A is reduced if A = Zk, or equivalently co(A) = co(A) and A is not contained in a hyperplane. We will typically denote regions of Rk with a tilde such as A  Rk. By abuse of notation, we will use | · | to refer both to cardinality of sets, and for volumes of sets. It will be clear with the tilde

2.2.2 Observations
The first observation guarantees that hyperplanes H have small intersections with discrete boxes. In particular, large subsets of B cannot be covered by few hyperplanes.
Observation 2.2.18. Given a hyperplane H and a box B = B(n1, . . . , nk), we have
|H  B|  min{ni}-1|B|.
In particular, a subset A  B with |A| > m min{ni}-1|B| cannot be covered by m hyperplanes.
Proof. If coordinate basis vector ei is not parallel to H, then the projection of H  B away from the ith coordinate injects into the same projection for B, and therefore |H  B|  n-i 1|B|.
The next observation is used to assume A is reduced so Convention 2.2.7 holds.
Observation 2.2.19. For all 0 > 0, the following holds. Given ni sufficiently large in terms of 0, for a subset A  B = B(n1, . . . , nk) with |A|  0|B|, we can find a subset A  B(2kn1, . . . , 2knk) such that A is reduced, |A| = |A|, dk(A) = dk(A), and |co(A) \ A| = | co(A) \ A|.
Proof. We first note that A is not contained inside a hyperplane by Observation 2.2.18. Take some a  A, then A - a  C := ki=1{-ni + 1, . . . , ni - 1}. The affine sub-lattice A-a is actually a subgroup v1, . . . , vk  Zk generated by linearly independent vectors vi = (vi,1, . . . , vi,k). Note that this property is robust to operations of the form: va gets swapped with vb or va gets replaced by va ± vb for some a = b.
Without loss of generality, suppose n1  . . .  nk. By performing a sequence of operations on the set of vectors {v1, . . . , vk}, we may assume that |v j,i| = 0 for all j > i, and |v j,i|  |vi,i| for

2.2 Definitions, Conventions, and Observations

all j  i. Indeed, let us explain how to deal with the first coordinate. As long as there exist a, b such that 0 < |va,1|  |vb,1|, we replace vb by vb - sign(va,1vb,1)va. This operation strictly decreases (|v1,1|, |v2,1|, . . . , |vk,1|) in lexicographic order, say. Thus, eventually this process must end. When this
happens all but one of the vectors vi must be null in the first coordinate. After we swap two of the
vectors we can ensure that v j,1 = 0 for all j  2. In a similar way, we can deal with all coordinates to ensure |v j,i| = 0 for all j > i. Note that this property is robust to operations of the form: va gets
replaced with va ± vb for some a < b. Because v1, . . . , vi are linearly independent, we have vi,i = 0.
Finally, as long as there exist a < b such that |va,b|  |vb,b| > 0, we replace va by va - sign(va,bvb,b)vb. Just as before, this process must end. When this happens we have |v j,i|  |vi,i| for all j  i.

We now prove an easy corollary of a lemma of Green and Tao [85]. For convenience, we include the lemma below. But before that we need a couple of definitions which we use only here.
A subset X  Nd is said to be an i-down-set if whenever we have that (x1, . . . , xd)  X, then (x1, . . . , xi-1, yi, xi+1, . . . , xd)  X for all integers 1  yi  xi. We say that X is a down-set if it is an i-down-set for each 1  i  d. In particular, the set B = B(n1, . . . , nd) is a down-set.
For each I  {1, 2, . . . , d} let I : Rd  Rd be the orthogonal projection I(x1e1 + . . . + xded) = iI xiei where e1, . . . , ed is the standard basis of Rd.

|A + B|  2d min(|A|, |B|) -  |I(X +Y )|.

We frequently need the following observation when considering A(+)A to show it has size roughly 2k|A| as described in Section 2.1.1. Observation 2.2.22. Let Y  (B) with B = B(n1, . . . , nk), and let 0 =v  {0} × {0, 1}k-1. Then

Proof. Consider all lines in the direction v intersecting (B). On each such line there are at most 2 values of x such that |{x, x +v}  co(Y )| = 1. Moreover, there are at most (k - 1) min{ni}-1|(B)| such lines which intersect (B) since each such line intersects (at least) two faces of the boundary of (B). Moreover, the intersection between the line and a face is a lattice point. Indeed, the i-th coordinate between two consecutive lattice points on such a line differs by one or zero.

2.3.1 Outline of the proof

We will create sets

A  A1  A2  A3  A4  A5  A+  A

(note that A5  A+) such that |A  A| is small, and A has a large number of properties which allow us to show that A is close to co(A). From this we will be able to conclude that A is close to co(A).

32

Sets in Zk with doubling 2k +  are near convex progressions

In Section 2.3.2, we derive a general reduction to sets for which the projection under  satisfies the induction hypothesis.
In Section 2.3.3, we construct A  A1  A2  A3 such that A3 is reduced, has large rows Rx close to co(Rx), and has (A3) close to co((A3)).
In Section 2.3.4, we construct A3  A4  A5 such that A5 has the same properties as A3 and the arithmetic progressions co(Rx) have the same step size d.
In Section 2.3.5, we show that d = 1, i.e., co(Rx) = co(Rx) is an interval for all rows Rx of A5. In Section 2.3.6, we show that filling in the rows of A5 to make a set A+  A5 preserves the properties that A5 had (this is the only step we deviate from throwing away a subset of rows). In Section 2.3.7, we show that we can approximate A+ with a subset A  A+ which has simultaneously
1. Few vertices on co(A)
2. (A) close to co((A))
3. The technical condition Observation 2.3.35.
Up to this point, we were able to show that |A  A+|   O(1)|A|. However obtaining A involves a double recursion, and we are only able to show |A  A| = o(1)|A| where o(1)  0 as   0.
In Section 2.3.8, we show that A is close to its convex hull. The key step is to convert the problem to one of bounding the size of the epigraph of a certain infimum-convolution of a function by the size of the epigraph of the original function.
Finally, in Section 2.3.9 we finish the proof of Theorem 2.1.4 by showing that A being close to its convex hull implies A is close to its convex hull.

2.3.7 Reductions Part 5: Approximating A+ by A  A+ with few vertices in co(A) and an extra technical condition
We now construct a set A  A+ with |A+ \ A| = o(1)|B|, |V (A) with the following properties. |V (A)|, which we recall is the number of vertices of co(A), is bounded by a function of  and | co((A)) \ (A)| = o(1)|(B)|. We would like A to posses an additional technical property. Intuitively, the desired property is that whenever the interval rows Rx, Ry, Rz, Rz+v of A satisfy x + y = z +v, Rx + Ry does not sit completely above or completely below Rz + Rz+v of the reference frame. This property is desirable because it allows us to switch between evaluating the sumset of a set to evaluating the infimum-convolution of a function. As usual we are not interested the in the entire sumset of A with itself, but rather in a very specific restricted sumset where each row Rx is summed with a bounded family of rows Ry with y  Y (x) and thus we are not interested in this property to hold globally but rather locally. To make this precise we need a few definitions.
Definition 2.3.21. For every A  B, let T +(A) (resp. T -(A)) be a triangulation of the upper (resp. lower) convex hull of co(A) with respect to the e1 direction, projected under  to {0} × Rk-1, so in particular every T  T +(A) has T  {0} × Rk-1. We ensure that if co(A1) = co(A2), then T +(A1) = T +(A2) and T -(A1) = T -(A2).
Notation 2.3.22. For a simplex T  {0} × Rk-1, we will write
T := T  {0} × Zk-1, and T  := T   ({0} × Zk-1)
where T  is the interior of T .
Definition 2.3.23. Given T  {0} × Rk-1 with integral vertices, and a set W  {0} × Zk-1, for every x  T we define the set
YW (x) := ((x + W )  T ) V (T )  T.
Definition 2.3.24. Given a collection M of sets WT  {0} × Zk-1 constructed for each simplex T  {0} × Rk-1 with integral vertices, we say that A  B has Property P(M ) if the following holds. For every T  T +(A)  T -(A), if x  T  \ (V (A)), y  YWT (x), z  (B) andv  {0} × {0, 1}k-1 with x + y = z + z +v and Rx + Ry, Rz + Rz+v nonempty, then (Rx + Ry)  (Rz + Rz+v) = 0/ .
Thus, the plan is to construct a set A  A+ with |A+ \ A| = o(1)|B|, which has simultaneously
1. |V (A)|, which we recall is the number of vertices of co(A), is bounded by a function of 
2. | co((A)) \ (A)| = o(1)|(B)|
3. A has Property P(M ) for a suitable family M of bounded sets WT constructed independently for all simplices T  {0} × Rk-1 with integral vertices (see Definition 2.3.34).

We show this using a double recursion, and the bounds we obtain will no longer be powers of  . In Part 1 of Section 2.3.7, we prove Proposition 2.3.25, which shows that we can ensure that 1
holds. This is accomplished by showing an analogous approximation result for (continuous) polytopes, and then transitioning to the discrete setting using Observation 2.2.24.
In Part 2 of Section 2.3.7, we prove Proposition 2.3.28, which shows that we can ensure that both 1 and 2 hold. Proposition 2.3.1 by itself shows that 2 holds, so we alternate applications of Proposition 2.3.25 and Proposition 2.3.1, and show that at some point both 1 and 2 hold simultaneously.
In Part 3 of Section 2.3.7, we prove Proposition 2.3.31, which shows that we can ensure that all of 1,2,3 hold. To do this, we show Proposition 2.3.30, which shows that we can ensure 1 and 3 hold. Similarly to the proof of Proposition 2.3.28, we alternate applications of Proposition 2.3.28 and Proposition 2.3.30, and show that at some point 1,2,3 hold simultaneously.
Finally, in the last part of Section 2.3.7, we apply Proposition 2.3.31 to A+ to construct A.

2.3.8 A is close to co(A)
In this section, we show that | co(A) \ A| = o(1)|B|. It is easy to show that

2k| co(A) \ A|  co(A + A) \ (A + A) + o(1)|B|.

Indeed, by (2.54) we have |A +A|  2k|A|+o(1)|B| and by Observation 2.2.21 we have 2k| co(A)|- o(1)|B|  | co(A) + co(A)|. Adding the two inequalities and rearranging the terms gives the desired inequality.
Therefore, it suffices to show that for some constant ck > 0 we have

co(A + A) \ (A + A)  (2k - ck )| co(A) \ A| + o(1)|B|

(2.57)

Indeed, by combining the two inequalities above, it follows that 2k| co(A) \ A|  (2k - ck)| co(A) \ A| + o(1)|B| which forces | co(A) \ A| = ck-1o(1)|B|. We now give a motivating outline.
For T  T +(A)  T -(A), we will define functions g+T , g-T : T  [0, n1] (actually we will need
[0, 2n1] for technical reasons), which encode the distances from the nonempty rows of A in T to the
upper and lower convex hulls of A respectively. Then we can estimate

| co(A) \ A| = o(1)|B| +    gT (x).
{+,-} T T  xT

(2.58)

Here is why one might expect the error term o(1)|B|. For a typical x  co((A)) we expect the interval row Rx of A to be non-empty and we also expect for each   {+, -} to exist a unique

2.3 Proof of Theorem 2.1.4 for k given Theorem 2.1.1 b) for k - 1

67

T   T  such that x  T . For such a typical x, the contribution from x in | co(A) \ A| is the total number of integer points above and below Rx contained inside co(A); the contribution from x in {+,-} TT  xT gT (x) is the total length of the intervals extending above and below Rx inside co(A). Note that for an interval, the length and the number of integer points inside it differ by at most 1. Therefore, a typical x contributes O(1) to the discrepancy. Hence, all the typical x contribute
O(1)|(B)| = o(1)|B| in total to the discrepancy.
An atypical x could potentially contribute up to O(n1) to the discrepancy. However, it turns out there are o(1)|(B)| such atypical x. To bound the number of points x contained inside at least two simplices T1, T2  T + note that x must be actually contained on the boundary of the two simplices. Moreover, the number of simplices in a triangulation of  co(A) is bounded in terms of  because the number of vertices of co(A) is bounded in terms of  . Furthermore, the number of points x in (B) contained inside a hyperplane is at most min(ni)-1n-1 1|B|. Therefore, all the atypical x contribute n1o(1)|(B)| = o(1)|B| in total to the discrepancy.
We will define functions g+T , gT- as certain restricted infimum convolutions of g+T and g-T with themselves. These will encode the distance between the rows of a certain subset of A + A (which we will guarantee to be intervals by Observation 2.3.35) to the upper and lower convex hulls of A + A
respectively. This subset accounts for almost all rows. Then we can similarly estimate

co(A + A) \ (A + A)  o(1)|B| +

Infimum convolution of functions

In this section we prove a general result about the infimum convolution (see Definition 2.3.40) of functions, related to the fact that small doubling implies being close to the convex hull.
Proposition 2.3.44. There exist constants ck, ck > 0 such that the following is true. Let T  (B) be a discrete simplex with integral vertices x0, . . . , xk-1  {0} × Zk-1, and let g : T  [0, 2n1] with g(xi) = 0 for all i. Then

We omit the subscript WT from now on. Throughout the entire proof we shall consider the sets Si, j = Si, j(T ) in Definition 2.3.32 with parameters i, j bounded above by µ1, µ2, respectively.

Definition 2.3.45. For a subset P  T , we define g(P) = xPT g(x), and for a subset Q  T + T , define g(Q) = xQ(T +T ) g(Q).
Definition 2.3.46. For a subset P  T , we define dk(P) = -g(P + P) + 2kg(P).

72

Sets in Zk with doubling 2k +  are near convex progressions

Let us give the high level overview of the proof Proposition 2.3.44. We want to show that
g(T )  ck-1dk (T ) + ck-1ck min{ni}-1|B|.
The first idea is to control the weight of g in (a dense family of) translates P of 2-iT . We show
g(P)  2-ikg(T ) + Oi(1)dk (T ) + Oi(1) min{ni}-1|B|.
Note that the factor in front of the dominant term one might initially guess is 2-i(k-1) rather than 2-ik, as the size of the domain over which we are summing is scaled by a factor of 2-i(k-1) but the function on this domain is unchanged. The bigger i, the bigger this anomaly is.
The second idea is to produce an efficient covering of T with translates P1, . . . , Ps of 2-iT . Of course, we always have  j |Pj|  |T |. We show there exist a covering such that
 |Pj| = O(1)|T |
j
Note that the inefficiency O(1) does not depend on the scale i and we get that the number of translates is s = O(1)2i(k-1).
Combining these two ideas we get
g(T )   g(Pj)  O(1)2i(k-1)(2-ikg(T ) + Oi(1)dk (T ) + Oi(1) min{ni}-1|B|).
j
Provided the scale 2-i is chosen sufficiently small, we get
g(T )  2-1g(T ) + O(1)dk (T ) + O(1) min{ni}-1|B|,
from which the conclusion follows immediately. Here we sketch the proof of the second idea. We may assume wlog T  {0} × Rk-1 is a regular
simplex. Let C  {0} × Rk-1 be a cube with |C| = |T |. Construct a cover of C with O(1) translates of T . This gives a cover of 2-iC with O(1) translates of 2-iT . In addition, construct a cover of T with (1 + o(1))2i(k-1) translates of 2-iC (which is possible since C is a cube with |T | = |C|). By combining the two covers, we obtain a cover of T with O(1)2i(k-1) translates of 2-iT .
Here we sketch the proof of the first idea in case k = 2. In this case T = {0} × [a, b] is an interval in {0} × Z. Assume for simplicity a and b are even such that P = {0} × [a, 2 a+b ] is a half size interval. In addition, assume for simplicity W = {0} × Zk-1.
Before we write the exact calculation let us give the intuition. Note that g is defined on the interval T + T which can be regarded as two copies of the interval T . Chapter 3
Sharp L1 inequalities for Sup-Convolution

The work in this section is joint with Peter van Hintum and Hunter Spink [97].

3.1 Introduction

In this chapter we prove Theorem 1.1.11 and Theorem 1.1.12, the sharp stability result for a linear Brunn-Minkowski inequality for hypographs of functions in low dimensions. For this class of geometric objects, Theorem 1.1.11 and Theorem 1.1.12 are strengthen versions of a conjecture of Figalli and Jerison [71]. For convenience, we introduce some notation and reformulate Theorem 1.1.11 and Theorem 1.1.12 as corollaries of Corollary 3.1.3 and Corollary 3.1.4, respectively.
Let C  Rk be a compact convex domain. For a bounded function f : C  R, the upper convex hull of f , denoted by co( f ), is defined to be the infimum of all concave functions larger than f , and for bounded measurable functions f1, . . . , fn : C  R, the sup-convolution is defined to be

( f1  . . .  fn)(z) := sup f1(x1) + . . . + fn(xn) : x1 + . . . + xn = z .

n

n

The operation of sup-convolution, or in its equivalent form inf-convolution -((- f1)  . . .  (- fn)),
naturally appears in problems of optimization, with f1, . . . , fn utility functions and C representing a cost domain [53]. For a general survey, see [150]. Clearly f1  . . .  fn  n f1+...+ fn , and equality is attained when for example f1, . . . , fn coincide with the same concave function f = co( f ).
We can view the sup-convolution operation geometrically in terms of the Minkowski sum of regions in Rk+1. Indeed, consider the hypograph

A f , = {(x, y)  C × R :   y  f (x)}.

Then we have the closed convex hull co(A f , ) = Aco( f ), , and for  sufficiently negative we have 1
A f1... fn, = (A f1, + . . . + A fn, ). n

92

Sharp L1 inequalities for Sup-Convolution

The study of how close a Minkowski sum is to its convex hull was started by Starr-Shapley-

Folkman [149] and Emerson-Greenleaf [54], who showed that if A1, . . . , An are subsets of the unit

ball

in

Rk,

The study of how close a Minkowski sum is to its convex hull is also closely related to the stability

of the Brunn-Minkowski inequality. The Brunn-Minkowski inequality says that if A and B are

inequality quantify how close A, B are to homothetic convex sets when |A + B| k  |A| k + |B| k .

The precise stability of the Brunn-Minkowski inequality for sets A, B restricted to various sub-

classes of geometric objects, is one of the central open problems in the study of geometric inequalities.

It has been explored intensively in recent years by Barchiesi and Julin [11], Carlen and Maggi [39],

Christ [44], Figalli and Jerison [67, 69, 71], Figalli, Maggi and Mooney [72] and Figalli, Maggi and

Pratelli [73, 74].

inequality for homothetic sets, we [98] showed that for t  (0, 1) there are ck(t), dk(t) > 0 such that for

In Section 2 we show that Theorem 3.1.1, Conjecture 3.1.5, Theorem 3.1.7, and Theorem 3.1.2

reduce to the case that C = T is a simplex, f = 0 on the vertices and f  0 otherwise. In Section 3 we construct our hypersimplex subdivision of T . In Section 4 we introduce a new geometric notion of

"m-averageable subsets of T ", and reduce to showing certain hypersimplices in T are "m-averageable".

In Section 5 we show that the relevant hypersimplices up to dimension 3 are "m-averageable" and

conclude Theorem 3.1.1 and Theorem 3.1.2. In Section 6 we prove Theorem 3.1.7. Finally, in Section

7 we show how the existence of a non-sharp constant in Conjecture 3.1.5 can be derived from [98],

and we also give a quick self-contained proof. We end the chapter with a brief discussion about

Conjecture 3.1.5 in Section 8.

Chapter 4
On the Erdos Covering Problem: The density of the uncovered set

The work in this section is joint with Paul Balister, Béla Bollobás, Robert Morris and Julian Sahasrabudhe and has been published in Inventiones mathematicae [6].

4.1 Introduction
In this chapter we develop a general method, which is based on that of Hough [100], for bounding the density of the set left uncovered by a collection of arithmetic progressions, which we use to prove Theorems 1.2.2 to 1.2.8 (we defer Theorem 1.2.7 to the next chapter). These results treat several well known open problems on covering systems of Erdos [55], Erdos and Selfridge [75], Schinzel [136] and Filaseta, Ford, Konyagin, Pomerance and Yu [76]. For convenience, we restate Theorems 1.2.2 to 1.2.8 here as Theorems 4.1.1 to 4.1.7.

Throughout the thesis we exclude the trivial arithmetic progression Z.

Theorem 4.1.1. Let  > 0 and let µ be the multiplicative function defined by

µ(pi) = 1 + (log p)3+ p

In the following results we do not allow a modulus to be 1.

Theorem 4.1.5. If A is a finite collection of arithmetic progressions that covers the integers, then at least one of the moduli divides another.

Theorem 4.1.6. If A is a finite collection of arithmetic progressions with distinct square-free moduli that covers the integers, then at least one of the moduli is even.

Theorem 4.1.7. Let A = {Ad : d  D} be a finite collection of arithmetic progressions with distinct moduli that covers the integers, and let Q = lcm(D) be the least common multiple of the moduli. Then either 2 | Q, or 9 | Q, or 15 | Q.

The rest of this chapter is organized as follows: in Section 4.2 we outline the sieve that we use in the proofs, and in Section 4.3 we state and prove our main technical results, Theorems 4.3.1 and 4.3.2. In Section 4.4 we complete the proof of Theorem 4.1.1, and in Section 4.5 we prove a variant of the main result of [76]. In Section 4.6 we explain how our sieve can be used to prove the non-existence of coverings sets with certain properties, and in Sections 4.7-4.9 we use this method to prove Theorems 4.1.4, 4.1.5 and 4.1.7 (we leave the proof of Theorem 4.1.6 to the next chapter). Finally, in Section 4.10, we provide the constructions for Theorem 4.1.2 and Theorem 4.1.3.

4.2 The Sieve

In this section we outline the proof of Theorem 4.1.1. We consider a finite set of moduli denoted by D  N \ {1} and a finite collection A = {Ad : d  D} of arithmetic progressions, where Ad = ad + dZ is an arithmetic progression with modulus d. The goal is to estimate the density of the uncovered set

R := Z \ Ad.
dD

Rather than considering the entire collection of progressions A all at once, we expose the

progressions `prime by prime' and track how the density of the uncovered set evolves. To be more

precise, let p1, p2, . . . , pn be the distinct prime factors of Q := lcm(D) (usually, but not necessarily,

listed in increasing order) so that

n
Q =  p jj
j=1

for some integers  j  1. Define, for each 0  i  n,

i
Qi :=  p jj
j=1

and write

Di := d  D : d | Qi

and Ai := Ad : d  Di

for the set of differences and the corresponding arithmetic progressions whose prime factorization

only includes the first i of these primes. (In particular, Q0 = 1 and D0 = A0 = 0/ .) Note that, although

lcm(Di) | Qi, we do not necessarily have lcm(Di) = Qi, since earlier primes can occur to higher

powers in later moduli. Let

Ri := Z \ Ad = Z \ Ad,

dDi

Ad Ai

be the set of elements not contained in any of the progressions of Ai, so that R0 = Z and Rn = R. We also write Ni := Di \ Di-1 for the set of "new" differences at the ith stage, and define

Bi := Ad
dNi

(4.3)

to be the union of the arithmetic progressions exposed at step i, so that Ri = Ri-1 \ Bi. It is convenient to consider Ri as a subset of the cyclic group ZQi (or of ZQ), which is possible
because for each d  Di the set Ad is periodic with period d | Qi. In particular, note that the density of Ri in Z is equal to the measure of the set Ri in the uniform probability measure on the finite set ZQi. During the proof we in fact need to consider non-uniform probability measures Pi on ZQi; note that each such measure can be extended (uniformly on each congruence class mod Qi) to a probability

110

On the Erdos Covering Problem: The density of the uncovered set

measure on ZQ.1 We borrow (and abuse) terminology from measure theory by calling a subset S  ZQ (or a Q-periodic set S  Z) Qi-measurable if S is a union of congruence classes mod Qi.
4.2.1 A sketch of the method
The basic idea is quite simple. We construct measures Pi in such a way that Pi(Bi) is small, but without changing the measure of B j for any j < i. It follows that the measure of Z \ R in the final measure Pn is at most i Pi(Bi), and thus if this quantity is less than 1, it follows that the arithmetic progressions do not cover Z.
To bound Pi(Bi), we use the 1st and 2nd moment methods (see Lemma 4.3.3, below). More precisely, we bound the expectation (in the measure Pi-1) of the proportion of the `fibre' F(x) =
(x, y) : y  Zpi of x  ZQi-1 removed in step i, and the expectation of the square of this quantity.
i
Bounding these moments is not too difficult, see Lemmas 4.3.6 and 4.3.7, below. Finally, to deduce a lower bound on the uncovered set in the uniform measure, P0, we need to
bound the average distortion Pn(x)/ P0(x). We design the measures Pi so that the `average' here (which we take in the Pn-measure) is enough (by a convexity argument) to give such a lower bound, see Lemma 4.3.5.

4.2.2 The probability measures Pi

We next define the non-uniform probability measures Pi, which are inspired by (but different in important ways to) a sequence of measures used in [100], and which play a key role in the proof

of Theorem 4.1.1. The rough idea is that we would like to distort the space so as to `blow up' the

uncovered set, but without increasing the measure of any single point too much. More precisely, we

define the measures inductively, choosing Pi so that it agrees with Pi-1 on Qi-1-measurable sets, is not too much larger that Pi-1 anywhere, and (subject to these conditons) is as small as possible on the set Bi, the union of the arithmetic progressions removed at step i.

First, let P0 be the trivial probability measure on ZQ0 = Z1, or (equivalently, by the comments above) the uniform measure on ZQ. Now fix 1, . . . , n  [0, 1/2], let i  [n], and suppose we have already defined a probability measure Pi-1 on ZQi-1. Our task is to define a probability measure Pi on ZQi .
In order to view Ri-1 and Ri as subsets of the same set, let us (by the Chinese Remainder Theorem)

represent ZQi as ZQi-1 × Zpi , and denote elements of ZQi by pairs (x, y), where x  ZQi-1 and y  Zpi .

i

i

We may view Ri-1 as a collection of `fibres' of the form F(x) = {(x, y) : y  Zpi }, noting that Pi-1
i

is uniform on each fibre, and view Ri as being obtained from Ri-1 by removing the points that are

contained in the new progressions of Ai \ Ai-1.

4.2.3 A more precise sketch of the method

Having defined the non-uniform probability measures Pi, we can give a high level overview of why the method works, for example, showing that in any covering system A = {ad mod d : d  D} with

distinct square free moduli, the minimum modulus is bounded. In this subsection we write pi for the

4.2.4 Novelty of the method
As mentioned in the introduction, our method is based on that of Hough [100], but is actually simpler in various important ways. The technical difference is that the sieve used here processes the primes one at a time whereas the sieve used in [100] processes the primes in large groups; the measures constructed here allow covered points to have some small weight whereas the measures constructed in [100] are supported on the uncovered set. As a consequence, our analysis is simpler: we do not need to appeal to the Lovász Local Lemma to estimate the density of the surviving set in each fiber and

4.3 A general theorem

113

we do not need to track any notion of quasi-randomness of the surviving set at each step. Moreover,
because we distort the measure in incremental steps, we are able to track the distortion between the uniform measure P0 and the final measure Pn. More precisely, we are able to track the 'average' distortion Pn(x)/ P0(x) (which we take in the Pn-measure) and thus we are able to estimate the density of the uncovered set not only in the measure Pn but also in the uniform measure which was not done in [100].

4.5 The Erdos-Graham Conjecture
As a simple consequence of Theorem 4.1.1, we will next give a new strengthening of the conjecture of Erdos and Graham [60] mentioned in the Introduction. Recall that in the original conjecture, which was confirmed by Filaseta, Ford, Konyagin, Pomerance and Yu [76] in 2007, the lower bound M on n was allowed to depend on K. The result proved in [76] required such a bound (n > K for some

4.6 A general method of applying the sieve

4.6 A general method of applying the sieve

In this section we describe a practical method of applying our method to problems involving covering systems, such as Schinzel's conjecture and the Erdos-Selfridge problem. More precisely, we will show how one can choose the constants i sequentially and optimally via a simple recursion which may be run on a computer. We will also give a simple criterion (see Theorem 4.6.1, below) which we prove is sufficient to deduce that the collection A does not cover the integers. Combining these (that is, running the recursion until the criterion is satisfied), we reduce the problems to finite calculations, which in some cases are tractable. To demonstrate the power of this approach, we will use it in Sections 4.7-4.9 to prove Theorems 4.1.5 and 4.1.7, and to significantly improve the bound on M in Hough's theorem.
We begin by defining a sequence of numbers fk = fk(A ), which will (roughly speaking) encode how "well" we are doing after k steps of our sieve. We remark that, from now on, we will perform a step of the sieve for every prime, whether or not it divides Q. We will therefore write pk for the kth prime, i.e., p1 = 2, p2 = 3, etc. Accordingly, we write Qi for the maximal divisor of Q with prime factors p1, . . . , pi. Let pn be the largest prime factor of Q.
Fix i0  N and fix a probability measure Pi0 on ZQi0 . Given parameters i0+1, . . . , n  [0, 1/2] and given probability measures Pi0+1, . . . , Pn on ZQi0+1, . . . , ZQn constructed recursively using (4.4) and (4.5) from the probability measure Pi0, define

fk = fk(A ) :=   1 +

4.7 The Erdos-Selfridge Problem
In this section we will prove Theorem 4.1.7, which is a simple consequence of the method described in the previous section. First, however, let us show how to prove the following (only slightly weaker) theorem, which was first proved by Hough and Nielsen [101].
Theorem 4.7.1. Let A be a finite collection of arithmetic progressions with distinct moduli, none of which is divisible by 2 or 3. Then A does not cover the integers.
Proof. Let P0 be the trivial measure on Z1. Set 1 = 2 = 0 and construct probability measures P1 and P2 recursively using (4.4) and (4.5) from the probability measure P0. 4.9 Schinzel's Conjecture

In this section we use the method of Section 4.6 to prove Schinzel's Conjecture [136], restated below.

Theorem 4.9.1. If 1 < d1 < d2 < · · · < dk are the moduli of a finite collection of arithmetic progressions that covers the integers, then di | d j for some i < j.
We will argue by contradiction, assuming that we have a set {d1, . . . , dk} of moduli of a covering system of the integers that forms an antichain under divisibility. Having completed the easy preliminaries, we are ready to prove Schinzel's conjecture.

Proof of Theorem 4.1.5. We first observe that we may assume that none of the moduli di are prime
powers. Indeed, we may assume that the covering is minimal, so the removal of any Adi results in a set of progressions that do not cover Z. If di = p j for some prime p and j > 0, then the prime can appear at most to the ( j - 1)st power in any other moduli. Thus the other progressions fail to cover
some congruence class mod Q/p, where Q = lcm{d1, . . . , dk}. But this congruence class cannot be covered by Adi as di  Q/p, a contradiction.

4.10 Constructions

In this section we will provide constructions of families of arithmetic progressions that answer (negatively) the question of Filaseta, Ford, Konyagin, Pomerance and Yu [76] mentioned in the Introduction, and show that Theorem 4.1.1 is not far from best possible. To be precise, we will prove Theorem 4.1.2 and Theorem 4.1.3.
Note that the bound (4.2) in Theorem 4.1.2 is (obviously) best possible; we remark that the moduli in our construction will moreover be square-free. Theorem 4.1.3 shows that the function (log p)3+ in the statement of Theorem 4.1.1 cannot be replaced by a constant.
The proof of Theorem 4.1.2 is relatively simple, while the proof of Theorem 4.1.3 will require somewhat more work.
