Chapter 5
The Erdos-Selfridge problem with square-free moduli
The work in this section is joint with Paul Balister, Béla Bollobás, Robert Morris and Julian Sahasrabudhe and is published in Algebra and Number Theory [5].
5.1 Introduction
In this chapter we refine the method from the previous chapter in order to prove Theorem 1.2.7, which states that in any covering system with distinct square free moduli there exists an even modulus. This resolves, in the square free case, a problem of Erdos and Selfridge [75] from 1965. For convenience, we restate Theorem 1.2.7 here as Theorem 5.1.1. Theorem 5.1.1. In any finite collection of arithmetic progressions with distinct square-free moduli greater than 1 that covers the integers, at least one of the moduli is even.
We prove Theorem 5.1.1 in a (slightly more general) geometric setting; a second aim of this chapter is to investigate covering systems in this setting. Let S1, . . . , Sn be finite sets with at least two elements, and set
Q = S1 × · · · × Sn. If A = A1 × · · · × An  Q with each Ak either equal to Sk or a singleton element of Sk, then we say that A is a hyperplane. We write A = [x1, . . . , xn], where xk  Sk  {} for each k  [n], and  indicates that Ak = Sk. Let us write F(A) = {k : xk  Sk} for the set of fixed coordinates of A, and say that two hyperplanes A and A are parallel if F(A) = F(A). Let us also say that A is non-trivial if F(A) = 0/ .
Theorem 5.1.1 is equivalent to the following theorem in this geometric setting. Theorem 5.1.2. For each k  [n], let pk be the kth prime, and set Sk = [pk+1]. Any collection of non-trivial hyperplanes that covers Q := S1 × · · · × Sn contains two parallel hyperplanes.

138

The Erdos-Selfridge problem with square-free moduli

To see the equivalence of Theorems 5.1.1 and 5.1.2, observe that, by the Chinese Remainder Theorem, there is a natural equivalence1 between finite collections A of arithmetic progressions with square-free, odd, pn+1-smooth moduli that cover the integers, and finite collections H of hyperplanes that cover the box Q = [p2] × · · · × [pn+1]. Moreover, if the moduli of A are distinct then the hyperplanes in H are non-parallel.
In order to motivate our second main theorem, let us next state, in this geometric setting, a special case (for square-free moduli) of the breakthrough result of Hough [100] which answered the Erdos minimum modulus problem.

Theorem 5.1.3 (Hough, 2015). Let p1, . . . , pn be the first n primes. There exists a constant C such that if A is a collection of hyperplanes that cover Q := [p1] × · · · × [pn], then either two of the hyperplanes are parallel, or there exists a hyperplane A  A with F(A)  [C].

To deduce Theorem 5.1.3 from the main result of [100], simply note that if every covering system

with distinct, square-free moduli contains an arithmetic progression with modulus at most M  pC, then the progression with minimum modulus corresponds to a hyperplane A with F(A)  [C]. In the

other direction, it follows from Theorem 5.1.3 that a covering system with distinct, square-free moduli

contains a progression corresponding to a hyperplane A with F(A)  [C], and the modulus d of this

progression

satisfies

d



C
i=1

pi.

Using our method, we are able to prove the following strengthening of Theorem 5.1.3.

Theorem 5.1.4. For every sequence of integers (qk)k1 such that qk  2 for each k  N and lim inf qk > 3, k k
there exists a constant C such that the following holds. Let A be a collection of hyperplanes that cover Q := [q1] × · · · × [qn] for some n  N. Then either two of the hyperplanes are parallel, or there exists a hyperplane A  A with F(A)  [C].

Note that in Theorem 5.1.3 the sequence (pk)k1 grows asymptotically as k log k, whereas in Theorem 5.1.4 we allow the sequence (qk)k1 to grow only linearly. We show (see Section 5.4) that Theorem 5.1.4 is close to best possible, since there exists an example with lim qk/k = 1 for which the conclusion of the theorem fails.
The rest of the chapter is organized as follows. In Section 5.2 we outline the sieve that we use in the proofs, and in Section 5.3 we state and prove our main technical results, Theorems 5.3.1 and 5.3.2. In Section 5.4 we deduce Theorem 5.1.4. Finally, we dedicate Section 5.5 to the proof of our main result, Theorem 5.1.1.
1To be precise, the progression a + dZ with d = iI pi corresponds to the hyperplane A = [x1, . . . , xn] where xi = a mod pi if i  I, and xi =  otherwise.

5.2 Definition of the Sieve

In this section we outline the proofs of Theorems 5.1.2 and 5.1.4. In particular, we generalize the method developed in Chapter 4 to the geometric setting, and while doing so we introduce several new ideas that turn out to be crucial in the proofs. For the convenience of the reader and for completeness, we include full proofs of all intermediate results, even though several of them are direct adaptations of the corresponding results in Chapter 4.
As in the Introduction, let S1, . . . , Sn be finite sets with at least two elements, set

Q := S1 × · · · × Sn,

and let A be a collection of non-trivial hyperplanes, no two of which are parallel. Set

F = F (A ) := F(A) : A  A  P([n]) \ {0/ },

R := Q \ AF .
F F
Rather than considering the entire collection of hyperplanes A all at once, we expose the hyperplanes dimension by dimension and track how the density of the uncovered set evolves. To be more precise, define, for each 1  k  n,

5.2.1 The probability measures Pk
The construction of the probability measures is similar to that in Chapter 4, and no significant new ideas
are needed. The main difference from Chapter 4 is that instead of starting with the uniform measure as our P0, we allow for possible optimization of the measure on the first few coordinates. In general we start with some measure Pa, to be determined, which is supported on Ra  Qa = S1 × · · · × Sa.2
Our aim is to construct, for each a < k  n, a measure Pk on Qk in such a way that Pk(Bk) is small, but without changing the measure of Bi for any i < k. Fix a sequence of constants a+1, . . . , n  [0, 1/2], and assume that we have already defined a probability measure Pk-1 on Qk-1. Recall that Qk = Qk-1 × Sk, and hence the elements of Qk can be written as pairs (x, y), where x  Qk-1 and y  Sk. We may view Rk-1 as a collection of fibres of the form Fx = {(x, y) : y  Sk}  Qk, where Pk-1 is extended uniformly to a measure on Qk (so is uniform on each fibre), and view Rk as being obtained from Rk-1 by removing Bk, i.e., by removing the points that are contained in the new hyperplanes of Ak \ Ak-1.

To motivate the definition above, note that if k(x)  k, then Pk(x, y) = 0 for every element of Qk that is covered in step k, and that the measure is increased proportionally elsewhere to compensate. Before embarking on the (straightforward) proofs of Theorems 5.3.1 and 5.3.2, let us briefly

to apply it, we first need to remove from A each of the codimension 1 hyperplanes, each of which is of the form S1 × · · · × Si-1 × {s} × Si+1 × · · · × Sn for some i  [n] and s  Si. Note that in doing
so we remove the point s from the possible values of the ith coordinate, effectively replacing Si by Si := Si \ {s}. After removing these hyperplanes, the remaining elements of A will all have at least two fixed coordinates, and can be assumed to be hyperplanes in Q = S1 × · · · × Sn , where Si = Si if {i}  F (A ). Removing the codimension 1 hyperplanes in this way makes a significant difference to our estimate on Mk(2), at the expense of (possibly) reducing each |Si| by 1. In practice, this turns out to often give better bounds on the removed measure.

5.4 Proof of Theorem 5.1.4
In order to deduce Theorem 5.1.4 from Theorems 5.3.1 and 5.3.2, it will suffice to show that there is an appropriate choice of C and 1, 2, . . . , n such that µn > 0.

5.5 The Erdos-Selfridge problem
In this section we will prove Theorem 5.1.2 (and hence also Theorem 5.1.1). To do so, we will again apply the sieve introduced in Section 5.2, but this time we will need to choose the various parameters much more carefully. In particular, we will deal with the primes in three groups: first the set {3, 5, 7, 11}, then the primes between 13 and 73, and finally the primes larger than 73. We will discuss these in reverse order, so as to motivate the bounds we prove.
Let B be a collection of non-trivial hyperplanes in P := [3] × [5] × · · · × [pn], no two of which are parallel. Our aim is to show that B does not cover P. To do so, we will in fact apply our sieve to a modified collection, obtained by removing the co-dimension 1 hyperplanes, as described after the statement of Theorem 5.3.2, for the primes p  p21 = 73.5 After doing so, we obtain a collection A of hyperplanes in Q = S2 × · · · × Sn, where |Sk| = pk - 1 for each 2  k  21, and |Sk| = pk for each 22  k  n, such that no two hyperplanes in A are parallel, and if F(A) = {i} for some A  A then i  22. We remark that we will use some results from Chapter 4 to deal with the large primes, and our indexing of the sets Sk is chosen to avoid a conflict with the notation used there. It will also be convenient (see Section 5.5.3, below) to assume (as we may) that A  B for any A, B  A with A = B.

5.5.3 Constructing the measure P5
It remains to construct a measure on the uncovered set R5  S2 × · · · × S5 obtained after removing all hyperplanes corresponding to arithmetic progressions whose moduli involve only the primes 3, 5, 7 and 11. By Corollary 5.5.2 and Lemma 5.5.3, it will suffice to prove the following lemma.
Lemma 5.5.4. For each A as above, there exists P5 such that c5(3) - 3c5(1)/4  9.019.
Proof. Let us write F for the collection of sets F  {2, 3, 4, 5} with |F|  2. For each set F  F , we need to choose a hyperplane AF with fixed set F, and for each such family we need to construct a measure P5 supported on the uncovered set. Not surprisingly, there are far too many configurations to deal with easily, so we need to make a few reductions.
Recall first that (by assumption) no hyperplane in A is contained in another. Also, we may assume there is a hyperplane for each F with |F|  2 as including extra hyperplanes only makes covering easier. However, it is not clear that we can make both assumptions simultaneously. Let us write F  F if iF 2i < iF 2i (i.e., F precedes F in colexicographic order). In studying all possible configurations, we shall go through the elements of F in colexicographic order and for each F  F we choose the hyperplane AF . In particular, if F appears before F then AF cannot be contained in AF . Moreover, if the hyperplanes AF preceding AF do not form a cover everything, then there exists a choice for AF such that AF covers a yet uncovered point, i.e., AF is not contained in any prior AF. Therefore, at each stage in order to ensure that there exists a valid choice for the hyperplane AF , we just need to check that the hyperplanes chosen so far do not cover everything. Moreover, we only need to study configurations `up to isomorphism', in the following sense. For each F  {2, 3, 4, 5} write AF = [a2,F , a3,F , a4,F , a5,F ] with ai,F  Si  {} so that AF  {xi = ai,F } when i  F and ai,F =  otherwise. Next, for a given F, order the choices of hyperplane AF lexicographically, so that AF  AF if ai,F < ai,F when i = min{ j  F : a j ,F = a j,F }. Now, suppose there exists a pair (F, i) such that

152

The Erdos-Selfridge problem with square-free moduli

i  F and ai,F  ai,F + 2 for every F  F with i  F. Then we can transpose ai,F and ai,F - 1 in Si to obtain an isomorphic configuration A  which is lexicographically smaller (with respect to the orders  and ), since AF  AF , but AF = AF for all F  F.
Applying these reductions reduces the number of configurations to 6,025,640,717 which, while it represents substantial progress, is still too large to conveniently construct optimized probability distributions for each configuration. However, the main contribution to the large number of configurations comes from the choice of the `last' few hyperplanes, namely A45, A245, A345, and A2345.8 For example, we might have as many as 2 × 4 × 6 × 10 = 480 choices for A2345, as we are selecting a single point in Q5 (although in practice the number of choices is reduced somewhat by the comments above). Ignoring the choices for A45, A245, A345, and A2345 reduces the number of configurations to just 7637, which is far more manageable.
Our strategy is therefore as follows. We first consider a choice of the hyperplanes, A23, A24, A25, A34, A35, A234 and A235, without including the last four hyperplanes A45, A245, A345 and A2345. where the cI are the bounds on c(I) given by the linear programming problem. We then check if the bound in (5.22) is less than 9.018.9 If so, then we proceed to the next configuration. There are 90 (out of 7637) configurations of (A23, . . . , A235) where this fails. For these, we loop through all choices of A45 and perform the above calculation with just A245, A345, and A2345 unspecified. From these 90 configurations we obtain 1083 configurations with A45 included, but for only 12 of these does our bound still exceed 9.018. These 12 give rise to 312 configurations including A245, of which 3 still exceed our bound. These 3 give rise to 216 configurations where we are forced to include A345, but only 2 which still exceed our bound. Finally, these 2 give 142 configurations where we are forced to include all the AF , but only two have c5(3) - 3c5(1)/4  9.018, and these are listed in Table 5.2. We deduce that for all choices of the hyperplanes {AF : F  F } in Q5 we can find a probability measure P5 on R5 such that c5(3) - 3c5(1)/4 < 9.018071.
9This bound was chosen, after some experimentation, to be just below the worst case value of c5(3) - 3c5(1)/4 given in Table 5.2 for configurations using all the hyperplanes.

154

The Erdos-Selfridge problem with square-free moduli

All calculations were performed in C using the Gurobi linear optimization package to solve the LP minimizations. With the strategy as described above, the calculations to determine the worst case configurations took about 40 seconds on a laptop. Source code and results can be found at [2].
As noted above, Theorem 5.1.2 follows immediately from Corollary 5.5.2 and Lemmas 5.5.3 and 5.5.4. In fact, since the results from Chapter 4 (which we used to deal with the large primes) did not require the assumption that the moduli are square-free, we actually proved something slightly stronger: if the moduli are distinct and each prime p  73 in their prime factorization occurs to a power at most 1, then at least one of the moduli must be even. In other words, the `square-free' condition is only needed on the 73-smooth part of the moduli. Of course, if we could reduce "p  73" to "p < 3" then the Erdos-Selfridge problem would be solved, so it would be interesting to see to what extent the bound 73 could be reduced.

Chapter 6
The structure and number of Erdos covering systems

The work in this section is joint with Paul Balister, Béla Bollobás, Robert Morris and Julian Sahasrabudhe [3].

6.1 Introduction

In this chapter we prove Theorem 1.2.10, the asymptotic formula for the logarithm of the number of minimal covering systems of a given size. This answers a question of Erdos [56] from 1952. For convenience, we restate Theorem 1.2.10 here as Theorem 6.1.1.

Theorem 6.1.1. The number of minimal covering systems of Z of size n is

4 n3/2 exp 3 + o(1) (log n)1/2

(6.1)

as n  , where

  =  log t + 1 2 .

t=1

t

We remark that proving a weaker upper bound, with a different constant in the exponent, is

significantly easier, and we give a short proof of such a bound in Section 6.6. Let us also note here

that we prove the lower bound under the additional restriction that the moduli are distinct, and so the

conclusion of Theorem 6.1.1 also holds for such systems.

In order to motivate the form of the formula (6.1), let us begin by describing a simple construction

that gives a slightly weaker lower bound. Let p1 < . . . < pk be the first k primes, and for each

(i)

(i)

i  [k], choose pi - 1 arithmetic progressions A1 , . . . , Api-1 with the following properties: for each

j  [pi - 1], the modulus of A j(i) is divisible by pi and divides Qi := p1 · · · pi, and A j(i) contains j · Qi-1.

It is not hard to see that, for each such choice, by adding the progression 0 (mod Qk) we obtain a

156

The structure and number of Erdos covering systems

distinct minimal covering system of size n = ki=1(pi - 1) + 1  k2 log k. Since we have 2i-1 choices for the progression A j(i) for each i  [k] and j  [pi - 1], this implies that there are at least

i=1

 n3/2 = exp (log n)1/2

minimal covering systems of Z of size n. In Section 6.5 we describe a somewhat more complicated construction that proves the lower bound in Theorem 6.1.1.
We refer to collections of progressions as in the construction above as "frames" (see Section 6.2 for a precise definition). The second main result of this chapter, and the key step in the proof of Theorem 6.1.1, is a structural description of all "efficient" covering systems; roughly speaking, we show that every such covering system contains a large "approximate frame". The purpose of the next section is to state this structural theorem.

6.2 The structure of efficient coverings

In this section we state our main structural theorem. In order to do so, it is convenient to shift our attention to the following (slightly more general) geometric setting. Let S1, . . . , Sk be finite sets with at least two elements and set SI := iI Si for each I  [k]. If H = H1 × · · · × Hk  S[k] with each Hi either equal to Si or a singleton element of Si, then we say that H is a hyperplane. We write F(H) := {i  [k] : |Hi| = 1} for the fixed coordinates of H, and F(A ) := HA F(H) if A is a collection of hyperplanes. We also write H = [x1, . . . , xk], where xi  Si  {} for each i  [k], and  indicates that Hi = Si.
Definition 6.2.1. A simple frame centred at an element (s1, . . . , sk)  S[k] (which we call the axis), is a sequence (F1, . . . , Fk), where Fi is a collection of |Si| - 1 hyperplanes of the form

x1, . . . , xi-1, a, , · · · ,  ,

(6.2)

Observe that if (F1, . . . , Fk) is a frame centred at (s1, . . . , sk), then the collection

A := F1  · · ·  Fk  [s1, . . . , sk]

is a minimal cover of S[k]. Indeed, if we remove the hyperplane x1, . . . , xi-1, a, , · · · ,  from A , then the element (s1, . . . , si-1, a, si+1, . . . , sk) is uncovered by the remaining hyperplanes. Note that if we set Si = {0, . . . , pi - 1} for each i  [k], then the construction given in the introduction is equivalent to a frame centred at (0, . . . , 0). When we (for now informally, but later on precisely) discuss frames in
Z, we always mean that each set Si = {0, . . . , p - 1} for some prime p (these primes are not generally distinct), and we map S1 × · · · × Sk into ZN, where N = i=1 k |Si|, using the Chinese Remainder

6.2 The structure of efficient coverings

157

Theorem to identify ZN with the product of groups Zp , and then expanding base p.1 Note that every arithmetic progression in Z corresponds to a hyperplane, but not every hyperplane corresponds to an arithmetic progression if primes are repeated (see Sections 6.5 and 6.7).
The key idea behind the proof of Theorem 6.1.1 is the following (imprecise) conjecture:

"Almost every minimal covering system of Z of size n is close to a frame."

We do not prove a result of this form; instead, we use a slightly weaker notion, which we call a  -generalized frame. These objects differ from frames in two key ways: the fixed elements "to the left" of i in a hyperplane H  Fi are allowed to vary with i, and instead of insisting that "all coordinates to the right are free" (as in (6.2)), we allow a few "small" coordinates to be fixed (with the product of their sizes bounded by 1/ ).
The next definition is both important and somewhat technical, and requires some additional notation. Given a hyperplane H, we write Hi for its ith coordinate, and for any I  [k] we write HI = iI Hi for the hyperplane in SI obtained by restricting H to the coordinates of I, and define µI(H) := |HI| · |SI|-1 when I = 0/ , and µ0/ (H) := 1.

Definition 6.2.2 ( -generalized frames). Let  > 0, and let S1, . . . , Sk be finite sets with at least two

elements. A simple  -generalized frame in S[k] is a sequence (F1, . . . , Fk), where Fi is a collection

of at most |Si| - 1 hyperplanes, satisfying the following conditions. For each i  [k], there exists a set

I(i)  {i + 1, . . . , k}, and for each j  I(i)  {i}, there exists an element s j(i)  S j, such that, for each

Moreover, if min |Si|, |S j|   -1 and i = j, then Fi and F j are disjoint. A  -generalized frame is obtained from a simple  -generalized frame by permuting the sets S1, . . . , Sk.

We are now ready to state our main structural theorem for covering systems that contain roughly (up to a constant factor) the same number of elements as a frame.

The theorem above can be thought of as an inverse theorem for the following extremal result of Simpson [140]. If A is a collection of arithmetic progressions, then we write lcm(A ) for the least common multiple of the moduli of the progressions in A .

In the Section 6.8, we provide (for the reader's convenience) a proof of Simpson's theorem. Let us also remark here that, while the form of the function  (C, ) does not matter for our purposes, we prove that Theorem 6.2.3 holds with  = (/C)O(log(1/)).
In order to deduce Theorem 6.1.1 from Theorem 6.2.3, we need to count  -generalized frames quite precisely, and show that there are relatively few choices for the remaining elements; we also need to show that there are few minimal covering systems that fail to satisfy (6.3). These calculations are carried out in Sections 6.6 and 6.7.

6.2.1 An outline of the proof of the structural theorem
The proof of Theorem 6.2.3 requires a few somewhat technical definitions, and to prepare the reader for these we begin by giving an outline of the argument. The idea is to construct a tree that encodes the structure of the covering system by `exploring' it coordinate by coordinate. To be more precise, given a minimal cover A of S[k], let us choose a coordinate i  [k] to explore, and observe (see Section 6.3.2 for the details) that for each s  Si we obtain a covering system of
S1 × · · · × Si-1 × {s} × Si+1 × · · · × Sk,
which we identify with S[k]\{i}. (Note that hyperplanes H  A with Hi = Si appear in each of the |Si| covering systems corresponding to coordinate i.) These covering systems may not be minimal, but for each s  Si we can take a minimal sub-covering As.
Now, some of the systems As may be trivial (i.e., may consist of a single hyperplane), and when this occurs we are happy, because such hyperplanes can be used in the frame that we are trying to construct. For the remaining elements s  Si, we consider the set of fixed coordinates F(As) of As, and observe (see Lemma 6.3.7) that every coordinate (except i) is in F(As) for some s  Si. We may now choose, for each s such that F(As) is non-empty, a coordinate j  F(As), and repeat the above construction, exploring the minimal covering system As, starting with the coordinate j. Iterating this process produces a rooted tree (which we call an `index tree', see Definition 6.3.1), each of whose vertices is labelled with a set I  [k] and a coordinate i  I, which are the fixed coordinates of the corresponding minimal covering system, and the coordinate `explored' at that vertex, respectively.

6.3 Exploring the cover

159

So far, we have not said anything about how to construct the sets Fi, or how to choose the coordinate i that we explore in a given step. For simplicity, let us explain this only for the first step (the choice for later steps is similar). First, if there exists i  [k] such that there are at least (1 - ) |Si| - 1 hyperplanes H  A with i  F(H) and µ[k]\{i}(H) >  , then we choose such a coordinate i to explore, and associate this collection of `frame-like' hyperplanes with the current vertex (in this case, the root of the tree). One of the key ideas of the chapter is that, if such a collection of hyperplanes does not exist for any i  [k], then we may use the Lovász Local Lemma to deduce (see Lemma 6.3.5) that there exists a coordinate j (which we will choose to explore), and a `large' collection G of hyperplanes in the current collection, such that j is a fixed coordinate of each. This collection of `garbage' hyperplanes will later be used, together with (6.3), to show that this case does not occur too often.
To see why the Lovász Local Lemma is useful in this context, consider the following simplified problem. Say we want to find an efficient cover A of [s]k with hyperplanes each with at least two fixed coordinates. Then there exists a coordinate j such that A j = {H  A : j  F(H)} satisfies |A j| >> s2. View [s]k as a probability space with the uniform probability measure µ. Note that two hyperplanes H1, H2 are independent events if and only if F(H1)  F(H2) = 0/ . If this is not the case write H1  H2. For each H  A let x(H) = exp(|F(H)|)µ(H). Then the local lemma implies that A is a cover only if there exists H  A such that µ(H)  x(H) HH(1 - x(H)). We deduce µ(H)  x(H) HH exp(-2x(H)) (if s >> e then x(H) << 1). We further deduce µ(H)  exp(|F(H)|)µ(H) exp(-2 HH exp(|F(H)|)µ(H)). This is equivalent to HH exp(|F(H)|)µ(H)  2-1|F(H)|. Finally, we deduce  jF(H) F(H) j exp(|F(H)|)µ(H)  2-1|F(H)|. By averaging, we get that there exist j  F(H) such that |A j| maxH exp(|F(H)|)µ(H)  2-1. As every hyperplane has at least two fixed point we conclude that exp(|F(H)|)µ(H)  (e/s)2 and |A j|  2-1e-2s2.
The plan described above is carried out in Section 6.3, the main result being Lemma 6.3.3,
which states that if  is sufficiently small, then there exists a suitable `exploration tree' T of A (see Definition 6.3.2). This exploration tree can be very large, however, and to extract our  -generalized
frame from it we need to choose a suitable sub-tree T . To do so, we choose k `special' vertices
of T, one for each coordinate, and take the union of the paths from these vertices to the root. If almost all of these special vertices are `good' (that is, we found a large collection of frame-like
hyperplanes when exploring them), then we obtain a sufficiently large  -generalized frame. On the
other hand, if a positive proportion of them are `bad', then we use the `garbage' hyperplanes to
show that inequality (6.3) cannot hold. In order to carry out this argument, we need to choose the
special vertices carefully; it turns out that it is sufficient to choose them via a depth-first search, see
Section 6.4 for the details.

6.4 Extraction of the frame, and the proof of Theorem 6.2.3
In order to prove Theorem 6.2.3, we will use the exploration tree T constructed in the previous section, together with the bound (6.3), to find a  -generalized frame for A . Roughly speaking, we would like to do this by choosing k vertices  (1), . . . ,  (k) such that the label of  (i) in T is i, define a tree T to be the union of the paths (in T) from  (i) to the root, and define the elements s j(i) using the elements su. For each good vertex  (i) we have a collection Fi of hyperplanes more or less as required, and for each bad vertex we obtain a large collection of `garbage' hyperplanes. We might therefore hope to use (6.3) to show that there are few bad vertices, and thus to deduce the bound (6.4).
There are two main problems with the strategy described above: the frame elements obtained for good vertices might not be disjoint, and each hyperplane might be included in the garbage set Gu for a very large number of bad vertices. We overcome both obstacles in the same way: by choosing the vertices  (i) via a depth-first search algorithm.
Here is the intuition. Assume that vertices  (i) and  ( j) are good, H  Fi F j and min(|Si|, |S j|)   -1. Say  (i) comes before  ( j) in the depth-first search algorithm. Then coordinate j is not explored anywhere on the branch extending to  (i). But coordinate j is in the set of fixed coordinates associated to  (i) as H is in the family of hyperplanes associated to  (i). This implies that j is explored on a branch extending further from  (i). As  ( j) is the first point in the depth-first search algorithm that explores coordinate j and  (i) comes before  ( j), this forces  (i) to be on the branch extending to  ( j). Thus, with respect to vertex  (i), hyperplane H has weight at most |S j|-1 <  , which contradicts the assumption that  (i) is a good vertex and H a frame element.
In Section 6.4.1 we will state precisely the object we will construct, and show that its existence implies the existence of a  -generalized frame. In Section 6.4.2 we will describe how we choose the sub-tree T  T, the frame elements (F1, . . . , Fk), and the `garbage' sets (G1, . . . , Gk); in Section 6.4.3, we will prove two lemmas on the disjointness of the frame elements and garbage sets; and in
Section 6.4.4 we will complete the proof of Theorem 6.2.3.

6.4.1 Tree-frames
The purpose of this section is to introduce the following somewhat complicated objects, which also provide significantly more information (though we will not need this) about the covering system. We complete each to a minimal cover of Z of size n by adding the progressions
2-1N (mod 2N) , for each   [t], and 0 (mod 2tN) . We obtain a family of
4 n3/2 exp 3 + o(1) (log n)1/2

minimal covering systems of Z of size n(x) + t = n, as required.

6.7 Proof of Theorem 6.1.1
In this section we will complete the proof of Theorem 6.1.1; we will begin by giving a sketch of the remaining part of the argument. Let A be a minimal covering system of Z of size n, let N = lcm(A ), and set S(p,e) = {0, . . . , p - 1} for each (p, e)  N. Let us map ZN into SN = (p,e)N S(p,e) in the natural way, i.e., using the Chinese Remainder Theorem, we map x  ZN to a vector y  SN where y(p,e) is the coefficient of pe-1 in the p-ary expansion of x modulo pe. Observe that the image of each progression H  A is a hyperplane in SN, and moreover, for each prime p, the set
(p, e)  N : (p, e)  F(H)
forms a (possibly empty) initial segment of the sequence (p, 1), (p, 2), . . . . We will say that the hyperplane H is arithmetic if it satisfies this condition.
We will apply Theorem 6.2.3 to A (with C = 4 and  > 0 an arbitrarily small constant), and deduce that either (6.3) fails to hold, or A contains an almost optimal  -generalized frame F(p,e) :

6.7 Proof of Theorem 6.1.1

179

(p, e)  N . In the former case we are done by Proposition 6.6.1, so let us assume the latter. We will carefully count the number of choices for the fixed sets of the frame elements F(p,e) such that p >  -1. The bound we obtain will be sufficiently strong unless N is primarily composed of primes smaller than  -1; however, for such N it turns out that the simpler argument used in Section 6.6 suffices to give a sufficiently strong bound.
Next, we bound the number of choices for the fixed sets of the remaining hyperplanes: those in frame sets F(p,e) for some prime p   -1, and those not used in the frame. Surprisingly, it turns out that we can again obtain a sufficiently strong bound using the method of Section 6.6. Roughly speaking, these `extra' hyperplanes are being used inefficiently, and would be better off (in terms of increasing the number of choices) by contributing to the construction of a larger frame (and thus a different value of N).
Finally, noting that the fixed sets of the hyperplanes in A correspond to the moduli of the original arithmetic progressions, we will use Lemma 6.6.2 to bound the number of minimal covering systems of Z of size n with given moduli.

Flat Littlewood Polynomials Exist

The work in this section is joint with Paul Balister, Béla Bollobás, Robert Morris and Julian Sahasrabudhe and is published in Annals of Mathematics [4].

7.1 Introduction

We say that a polynomial P(z) of degree n is a Littlewood polynomial if
n
P(z) =  kzk,
k=0
where k  {-1, 1} for all 0  k  n. The aim of this chapter is to prove the following theorem, which answers a question of Erdos [63, Problem 26] from 1957, and confirms a conjecture of Littlewood [112] from 1966.

Theorem 7.1.1. There exist constants  >  > 0 such that, for all n  2, there exists a Littlewood

polynomial P(z) of degree n with





 n  |P(z)|   n

(7.1)

for all z  C with |z| = 1.

Polynomials satisfying (7.1) are known as flat polynomials, and Theorem 7.1.1 can therefore be more succinctly stated as follows: "flat Littlewood polynomials exist". It turns out that our main challenge will be to prove the lower bound on |P(z)|; indeed, explicit polynomials satisfying the upper bound in (7.1) have been known to exist since the work of Shapiro [138] and Rudin [128] over 60 years ago (see Section 7.3). In the 1980s a completely different (and non-constructive) proof of the upper bound was given by Spencer [141], who used a technique that had been developed a few years earlier by Beck [12] in his study of combinatorial discrepancy. We remark that the Rudin-Shapiro construction, and also ideas from discrepancy theory (see Section 7.4), will play key roles in our proof.

188

Flat Littlewood Polynomials Exist

The study of Littlewood polynomials has a long and distinguished history (see, for example, [29] or [123]). The roots of their study go back to the work of Hardy and Littlewood [91] on Diophantine approximation over 100 years ago, the work of Bloch and Pólya [24] on the maximum number of real roots of polynomials with restricted coefficients, and the work of Littlewood and Offord [109, 115, 116] and others [61, 132] on random polynomials. Two important extremal problems that arose from these early investigations are Littlewood's L1-problem [92], which was famously resolved (up to constant factors) in 1981 by McGehee, Pigno and Smith [122] and Konyagin [107], and Chowla's cosine problem [43], see [30, 130].
The question studied in this chapter was asked in 1957, by Erdos [63], and was then taken up (and extensively studied) by Littlewood [110, 111, 113, 112] in a series of papers on the extremal properties of polynomials with restricted coefficients. In particular, Littlewood conjectured in [112] that flat Littlewood polynomials exist. This problem was given particular consideration in his well-known 1968 monograph [114], where he lays out 30 of his favourite problems, which he had curated for himself and his students for more than 30 years. In the book, he details several failed approaches to the "fascinating" question of the existence of flat Littlewood polynomials, and notes that he tried in vain to construct such polynomials.
Another well-known open problem, also proposed by Erdos [63] in the same problem paper, asks whether there exists a constant c > 0 such that, for every polynomial Pn(z) = k=0 n akzk with ak  C and |ak| = 1 for all 0  k  n, we have
 |Pn(z)|  (1 + c) n

for some z  C with |z| = 1. (Note that, by a simple application of Parseval's theorem, the conclusion holds with c = 0.) Let us write Fn for the family of Littlewood polynomials of degree n, and Gn for the

(larger) family with coefficients satisfying |ak| = 1. The class Gn is significantly richer than Fn, and

for polynomials in this richer class, significant progress was made in the years following Littlewood's

work. It had been known since the work of Hardy and Littlewood [91] that the upper bound in (7.1)

holds for the polynomial in Gn given by setting ak := kik, and Littlewood [111] proved that the

polynomial in Gn given by setting ak := exp k+1 2 i/(n + 1) satisfies the stronger upper and lower

bounds



|P(z)| = 1 + o(1) n

(7.2)

for all z  C with |z| = 1 except in a small interval around z = 1. Following further progress in [21, 38], and building in particular on work of Körner [108], the second question of Erdos [63] mentioned above was answered by Kahane [104], who proved that there exist ultra-flat polynomials in Gn, i.e., polynomials that satisfy (7.2) for all z  C with |z| = 1. More recently, Bombieri and Bourgain [32] improved Kahane's bounds, and moreover gave an effective construction of an ultra-flat polynomial in Gn.
For the more restrictive class of Littlewood polynomials, much less progress has been made over the past 50 years. The Rudin-Shapiro polynomials mentioned above satisfy the upper bound in (7.1)

7.2 Outline of the proof

189

with  = 2 when n = 2t - 1, and with  = 6 in general (see [1]). However, the previously bestknown lower bound, proved by Carroll, Eustice and Figiel [40] via a simple recursive construction, states that there exist Littlewood polynomials Pn(z)  Fn with |Pn(z)|  n0.431 for all sufficiently large n  N. Moreover, exhaustive search for small values of n (see [124]) suggests that ultra-flat Littlewood polynomials most likely do not exist. Let us mention one final interesting result in the direction of Littlewood's conjecture, due to Beck [16], who proved that there exist flat polynomials in Gn with a400 k = 1 for every k.
In the next section, we outline the general strategy that we use to prove Theorem 7.1.1. Roughly speaking, our Littlewood polynomial will consist (after multiplication by a suitable negative power of z) of a real cosine polynomial that is based on the Rudin-Shapiro construction, and an imaginary sine polynomial that is designed to be large in the (few) places where the cosine polynomial is small. To be slightly more precise, we will attempt to "push" the sine polynomial far away from zero in these few dangerous places, using techniques from discrepancy theory to ensure that we can do so. In order to make this argument work, it will be important that the intervals on which the Rudin-Shapiro construction is small are "well-separated" (see Definition 7.2.2, below). The properties of the cosine polynomial that we need are stated in Theorem 7.2.3, and proved in Section 7.3; the properties of the sine polynomial are stated in Theorem 7.2.4, and proved in Section 7.5.

7.2 Outline of the proof

We may assume that n is sufficiently large, since the polynomial 1 - z - z2 - · · · - zn has no roots with
|z| = 1 if n  2. It will also suffice to prove Theorem 7.1.1 for n  0 (mod 4), since the addition of a constant number of terms of the form ±zk can at worst only change |P(z)| by an additive constant. We can also multiply the polynomial by z-2n so that it becomes the centred `Laurent polynomial'

k=-2n
where n = 4n. The following theorem therefore implies Theorem 7.1.1.

Theorem 7.2.1. For every sufficiently large n  N, there exists a Littlewood polynomial P(z) =

for all z  C with |z| = 1.

We remark that the constants in Theorem 7.2.1 could be improved somewhat, but we have instead chosen to (slightly) simplify the exposition wherever possible.

7.2.1 Strategy

Before embarking on the technical details of the proof, let us begin by giving a rough outline of the strategy that we will use to prove Theorem 7.2.1. The first idea is to choose a set C  [2n] = {1, . . . , 2n}, and set -k = k for each k  C, and -k = -k for each k  S := [2n] \ C. Setting z = ei , the polynomial P(z) then decomposes as

2n

 kzk = 0 + 2  k cos(k ) + 2i  k sin(k ).

k=-2n

kC

kS

The real part of this expression is a cosine polynomial, while the imaginary part is a sine polynomial. 
Our aim is to choose the sine and cosine polynomials so that both are O( n) for all  , and so that the sine polynomial is large whenever the cosine polynomial is small.
Let us first describe our rough strategy for choosing the sine polynomial s( ), given a suitable 
cosine polynomial c( ). For each `bad' interval I  R/2Z on which |c( )| <  n, we will choose a direction (positive or negative), and attempt to `push' the sine polynomial in that direction on that
 interval. In other words, we pick a step function that is ±K n on each of the bad intervals, and zero elsewhere, where K is a large constant. We then attempt to approximate this step function with a
 sine polynomial, the hope being that we can do so with an error of size O( n) on each bad interval (independent of K).
In order to carry out this plan, we will use an old result1 of Spencer [141] on combinatorial discrepancy (in the form of Corollary 7.4.2 below), first to choose the step function, and then to show that we can approximate it sufficiently closely. More precisely, the first application (see Lemma 7.5.3) provides us with a step function whose Fourier coefficients are all small, and the second application (see Lemmas 7.5.4 and 7.5.5) then produces a sine polynomial that does not deviate by more that
 O( n) from this step function.
To make the sketch above rigorous, we will need the bad intervals to have a number of useful properties; roughly speaking, they should be `few', `small', and `well-separated'. In particular, we will construct (see Definition 7.2.2 and Theorem 7.2.3) a set I of intervals, each of size O(1/n),
 separated by gaps of size (1/n), with |c( )|   n for all  / II I. Moreover, the number of intervals in I will be at most n for some small constant  > 0.
To see that these demands are not unreasonable, note first that if C  [n] then the cosine polynomial has few roots, and the `typical' value of the derivative of the cosine polynomial should be ((n)3/2). This means that, if we choose  much smaller than , the polynomial should typically
 vary by more than  n over a distance of order 1/n. In particular, we will show that if the set of bad intervals cannot be covered by a collection of small and well-separated intervals (in the sense described above), then several of the derivatives must be small simultaneously. For our cosine polynomial we shall use an explicit construction based on the Rudin-Shapiro polynomials (see Section 7.3), and we
1We will in fact find it convenient to use a variant of Spencer's theorem, due to Lovett and Meka [117].

7.2 Outline of the proof

will show (see Lemma 7.3.5) that the value and first three derivatives of this polynomial cannot all be simultaneously small.

7.3 Rudin-Shapiro Polynomials

In this section we will define the cosine polynomial that we will use to prove Theorem 7.2.3, and the sine polynomial that we will use on the remaining even integers. In both cases, we use the so-called Rudin-Shapiro polynomials, which were introduced independently by Shapiro [138] and Rudin [128] (and whose sequence of coefficients was also previously studied by Golay [81]). These polynomials have been extensively studied over the last few decades, see, e.g., [35-37, 127]. Let us begin by recalling their definition.
Definition 7.3.1 (Rudin-Shapiro polynomials). Set P0(z) = Q0(z) = 1 and inductively define
Pt+1(z) = Pt (z) + z2t Qt (z), and Qt+1(z) = Pt (z) - z2t Qt (z),

for each t  0.

Observe that Pt(z) and Qt(z) are both Littlewood polynomials of degree 2t - 1. A simple induction argument (see, e.g., [123]) shows that Pt(z)Pt(1/z)+Qt(z)Qt(1/z) = 2t+1 for all z  C\{0}. It follows

that

|Pt (z)|2 + |Qt (z)|2 = 2t+1,

(7.5)

and hence |Pt(z)|, |Qt(z)|  2(t+1)/2, for every z  C with |z| = 1. Observing that the first 2t terms of Pt+1 are the same as for Pt, let us write P<n(z) for the polynomial of degree n - 1 that agrees with Pt(z) on the first n terms for all sufficiently large t, and note that Pt(z) = P<2t (z). The following bound, which is a straightforward consequence of (7.5), was proved by Shapiro [138]. (Stronger bounds are known, see [1], but we shall not need them.)

7.4 Minimising Discrepancy
In this section we recall the main `partial colouring' lemma of Spencer [141] (whose proof, as noted in the introduction, was based on a technique of Beck [12]), which will play an important role in the proof of Theorem 7.2.4. In particular, we will use the results of this section both to choose in which direction we should `push' the sine polynomial on each interval I  I , and to show that we can choose k  {-1, 1} so that it is pushed (roughly) the correct distance. Remark 7.5.10. We note that it is important that the lengths of the intervals I  I are multiples of n . Chapter 8
Judiciously 3-partitioning 3-uniform hypergraphs

The work in this section is joint with Hunter Spink and is published in Random Structures and Algorithms [142].

8.1 Introduction

Judicious partitioning problems seek to partition the vertices of a hypergraph H with m edges such

that various quantities are simultaneously maximized. The first such problem was treated by Bollobás

and Scott [26], where they proved that one can partition the vertices of a graph with m edges into k

parts

such

that

each

part

contains

at

most

m k2

+ o(m)

edges.

They

later

proved

[27]

that

the

vertices

of

a

3-uniform

hypergraph

can

be

partitioned

into

k

parts

each

of

which

contains

at

most

m k3

+ o(m)

edges.

Recently Hou, Wu, Zeng and Zhu [99] claimed to have shown the result for 4-uniform hypergraphs

with

m k4

+ o(m)

edges,

but

their

key

technical

lemma

is

incorrect.

The judicious partitioning problem we consider involves partitioning the vertices of an r-uniform

hypergraph with m edges into k parts so that the minimum number of edges touched by any part is maximized. Bollobás and Scott [28] conjectured that this maximum is (1 - (1 - 1k )r)m + o(m),1 which if true is optimal up to the error term by considering the complete r-uniform hypergraph.

Special cases of this problem have garnered considerable interest, particularly the case r = k = 3.

Bollobás, Reed and Thomason [25] proved every 3-uniform hypergraph H has a vertex-partition

V (H ) = V1 V2 V3 such that each part meets at least 13 (1 - 1e )m edges, which was claimed to have

been

improved

to

5m-1 9

by

Bollobás

and

Scott

[28]

(although

their

proof

apparently

contains

a

subtle

error, see Haslegrave [93]). Halsegrave [93] proved the optimal bound 0.6m, which is best possible

for small values of m. For large values of m, Ma and Yu [119] improved asymptotically the bound to

0.65m + o(m).

1We

note

that

this

asymptotically

strengthens

a

previously

conjectured

bound

of

rm 2r-1

for

any

m

when

r

=

k

by

Bollobás

and Thomason (see [25] and [28]).

214

Judiciously 3-partitioning 3-uniform hypergraphs

Here we resolve the conjecture of Bollobás and Scott in this special case.

Theorem 8.1.1. Every 3-uniform hypergraph H has a vertex-partition V (H ) = V1 V2 V3 such that each part meets at least 1927 m - O(m6/7) edges.

Our proof follows a strategy of Bollobás and Scott [27] and Ma and Yu [119]. The key technical

lemma

in

[119]

cannot

be

optimized

sufficiently

to

attain

27 19 ,

in

fact

cannot

be

pushed

past

0.7

<

19 27

=

0.703. We make an additional observation which imposes an additional inequality in this technical

lemma.

We now outline the strategy. It would be nice if for a given 3-uniform hypergraph, the uniformly random 3-partition of the vertices worked. Each part would touch in expectation 1297 m+o(m) edges, but the presence of high degree vertices prevents these three quantities from simultaneously concentrating

around the mean. For example, if there is a pair of vertices which belong to every edge, then a part

which

contains

neither

vertex

touches

with

high

probability

only

1 3

m

+

o(m)

edges.

To

avoid

this

situation, we partition the vertices according to a two stage process.

We first partition "high" degree vertices V high  V (H ) into three parts V1,V2,V3, and then take the remaining "low" degree vertices V low = V (H ) \V high and assign each of these vertices independently

to Vi with some probability pi with p1 + p2 + p3 = 1, so that the expected number of edges touched in each part is at least 1297 m + o(m). Because we exclude the high degree vertices from the random process, we have a tight concentration around the mean in each part by the Azuma-Hoeffding inequality (or

more specifically the version of the inequality due to McDiarmid [121]), and we conclude.

The threshold for "high" degree vertices and the application of the Azuma-Hoeffding inequality

are essentially automatic (if we only desire an o(m) error, we only have to ensure that the threshold

for "high" degree is o(m)). The challenge is to appropriately partition the "high" degree vertices so

that the pi can be chosen to make each part touch the correct number of edges in expectation. As in [119], after choosing the high degree threshold, we partition V high as follows. Denote by
E(H )i for i  {0, 1, 2, 3} to be the edges in E(H ) which touch i vertices in V high and 3 - i vertices in V low. The edges in E(H )2 induce a multi-graph G with vertex set V high obtained by replacing each edge e with e V high. Denote by xi the number of edges of G within Vihigh, and bi j the number of edges of G with endpoints in Vihigh and Vjhigh. We choose our partition V high = V1high V2high V3high so that b23 + b13 + b12 is maximal (equivalently x1 + x2 + x3 is minimal). The only facts used in [119] about

this partition were that bi j  max(2xi, 2x j). These inequalites follow by noting that the multigraph between Vihigh and Vjhigh maximizes the number of edges between the two parts for fixed Vihigh Vjhigh.

8.2 The technical lemma, its contrapositive, and our strategy

215

However, these numerical constraints fail to rule out the possibility that G is (close to) a configuration which is complete in Vkhigh, and complete bipartite between Vkhigh and the other two parts, which prevents the method from proving a bound exceeding 0.7m. We identify the additional inequality that if G maximizes b23 + b13 + b12, then bi j  12 xk, where k is the index = i, j. This arises from considering the effect of moving all vertices from Vihigh to Vjhigh, decreasing b23 + b13 + b12 by bi j, and dividing Vkhigh between Vkhigh and Vihigh in such a way that bik is maximized, increasing b23 + b13 + b12 by at least 12 xk.
The core of this chapter is a rather technical lemma. This technical lemma is used to show that if we subdivide V high so that b23 + b13 + b12 is maximal, then we can find probabilities p1, p2, p3 as described earlier. Given an instance of H , finding appropriate values of pi amount to simply solving cubic equations. Hence the laborious method used to prove the inequality is not reflected in the elegance of the final algorithm. We would be very interested in a less opaque derivation of the technical lemma.
The technical lemma is a system of inequalities with 13 variables and 11 degrees of freedom. We ultimately reduce this system to systems of inequalities with 6 variables and 2 degrees of freedom. For the systems which are not tight, we finely subdivide our parameter space and use a computer to check the system on each box. We prove all remaining systems of inequalities in the body of the chapter.

Keeping the qi fixed, we repeatedly linearly perturb the remaining variables in such a way that L stays constant and L1, L2, L3 do not decrease. We then perturb in this direction until one of the constraints attains equality. We then merge the associated variables (or set the corresponding variable to 0 if a variable hits zero), and repeat.
By linear algebra, it is clear we can do this (e.g., by choosing any perturbation fixing L, L1, L2 and choosing the sign so that L3 does not decrease), until the number of variables besides q1, q2, q3 is 3. Chapter 9
Multicolour chain avoidance in the
Boolean lattice
The work in this section is joint with Hunter Spink and is published in the European Journal of Combinatorics [143].
9.1 Introduction
The method of hypergraph containers, recently developed by Balogh, Morris and Samotij [7] and independently by Saxton and Thomason [134], inspired by the previous work of Conlon and Gowers [46], is an essential tool for counting independent sets in hypergraphs. Many natural problems can be phrased in this way, with the most direct applications toward determining the number of graphs on n vertices which avoid a collection of subgraphs. We refer the reader to the survey [8].
Recently, Balogh, Treglown and Wagner [10] showed that graph containers could be used in the Boolean lattice (P([n]), ) to give an alternate proof of Kleitman's result [105] counting the number of antichains in P([n]) (we note that the proof in [105] precedes the notion of graph containers, but has similar ideas). Using the hypergraph container algorithm, independently Collares and Morris [45] and Balogh, Mycroft, and Treglown [9] were able to further count the number of antichains in a random subset of P([n]), from which they were able to deduce the approximate size of the largest antichain therein.
Because every k-chain free set can be partitioned into k - 1 antichains, the graph container lemma (i.e., for 2-uniform hypergraphs) suffices not only to count k-chain free sets, but also to create a small collection of small sized containers for k-chain free sets via a product construction. However, [45] directly constructs a set of hypergraph containers without exploiting this observation as an application of the recent advances in the hypergraph container lemma through balanced supersaturation results.
In this chapter we answer analogous questions in a weighted coloured setting by building on [45]'s demonstration of the hypergraph container lemma through balanced supersaturation in P([n]). Fix colours 1, 2, . . . , m. Given a poset P, a colouring of P is a function c : P  {1, . . . , m}. A coloured

234

Multicolour chain avoidance in the Boolean lattice

poset (P, c) is a poset equipped with a colouring. A coloured subset of a poset P (resp. coloured poset (P, c)) is a pair (S, c) with S  P and c a colouring of S (resp. the colouring of S agreeing with c). Let G be a collection of coloured chain posets of lengths at least 2. Denoting disjoint union by , we write
G := G2  G3  . . .  Gk
where Gi contains exclusively chains of length i. We say that a coloured subset (S, c) of P([n]) avoids all configurations from G , or is valid with respect to G , if no element of G appears as a coloured subchain of (S, c). The present work is motivated by the following questions.

Question 9.1.1. What is the cardinality of the collection (G , n) (where the dependency on the number of colours m is suppressed here and throughout the chapter) of validly coloured subsets (S, c) of P([n]) with respect to G ?
Question 9.1.2. Let p1, . . . , pm  (0, 1] be such that i=1 m pi  1. If we colour each element of P([n]) independently with colour ci with probability pi and leave it uncoloured with probability 1 -  pi, then what is the expected number of validly coloured subsets (S, c) of (the coloured part of) P([n]) with respect to G ?

Multicoloured hypergraph container problems were only considered quite recently in the work of Falgas-Ravry, O'Connell, and Uzzell [64]. There it was shown that for a wide variety of coloured configuration avoidance problems, if there is a validly coloured subset using all but a o(1) proportion of the vertices, then the number of validly coloured subsets can be estimated quite precisely (see Theorem 9.1.8).
The questions we consider are the first instances of coloured hypergraph container problems that we are aware of which work in the presence of a "sparse" extremal example. A separate interesting feature is that the hypergraph we work with is not uniform and no uniformity dominates, so when we iteratively apply the container algorithm we may have to use potentially different uniformities at each stage.

Example 9.1.3. We now describe some instructive examples. A coloured chain with colours a1, . . . , a in increasing order is denoted a1  . . .  a.

1. Suppose we have 1 colour, i.e., m = 1, and that we have only one forbidden chain G =

{(1  1  . . .  1)}. Then (G , n) is the collection of k-chain free sets of P([n]), and by

k
[105, 10, 45] we have

|(G , n)| = 2(k-1)(nn/2)(1+o(1)).

2. Suppose that we have 4 colours, and let G be the 13 coloured 2-chains

· Consider the family of coloured subsets (S, c) such that the elements of S have sizes in

In general, the "dense" cases of Question 9.1.1 and Question 9.1.2 (see Remark 9.1.9) are solved by [64]. This occurs exactly when G does not contain a monochromatic chain of every colour. We are able to complete the analysis of these questions in the remaining "sparse" cases.

9.1.1 Preliminary definitions and main results
We recall from [64] some basic definitions of multicolour hypergraphs in our context. Definition 9.1.4. For a poset P, a template is a function

T : P  P({1, . . . , m})

from P to subsets of colours. Say that a template T is supported on A  P if T (x) = 0/ whenever x  A, and define Supp(T ) to be the smallest set on which T is supported.
Definition 9.1.5. For a template T on a poset P, we say a coloured subset (A, c) of P([n]) is contained in T if c(x)  T (x) for all x  A (note that such an A is contained in Supp(T )). We say that T is valid with respect to G if for every coloured subset (A, c) contained in T , no element of G appears as a coloured subchain of (A, c).
Definition 9.1.6. For a template T on a poset P, denote by

(T ) =  log(1 + |T (x)|),
xP
so that e(T) is the number of coloured subsets contained in T . We typically consider templates on the poset P = P([n]).
Remark 9.1.7. The reason we consider valid templates is that they provide a lower bound on the number of valid configurations

|(G , n)|  max  (1 + |T (x)|) = emaxT valid (T ).

One would hope that this is the correct bound up to a (1 + o(1)) factor in the exponent. The following theorem of [64], gives the sharp exponent in the "dense" case. Theorem 9.1.8. [64, Corollary 3.34] Define the maximal entropy of G to be
1 (G ) = lim sup n max (T ).

We have

|(G , n)| = e2n((G )+o(1)).

Remark 9.1.9. As mentioned earlier, this theorem correctly estimates |(G , n)| up to a 1 + o(1) factor in the exponent when (G ) > 0. We refer to this as the dense case, and it occurs precisely when G contains a monochromatic chain of every colour. In the sparse case when (G ) = 0, the upper bound given by Theorem 9.1.8 is trivial and does not estimate the correct exponent up to a 1 + o(1) factor.

9.1 Introduction

237

We solve Question 9.1.1 in the sparse cases by estimating |(G , n)| up to a 1 + o(1) factor in the exponent. This occurs when (G ) = 0, i.e., G contains a monochromatic chain of every colour. Note that in this case, there exists L = L(G ) such that no validly coloured set (S, c) contains a chain of length L, and we may easily deduce a crude upper bound of |(G , n)|  (m + 1)(L-1)(nn/2)(1+o(1)) by using the L-chain containers constructed from either of [10, 45] (see Section 9.4).
The following is one of our main theorems, solving the sparse cases of Question 9.1.1.
Theorem 9.1.10. Suppose that G contains a monochromatic chain of every colour, and define the constant (independent of n for large enough n)

crit = max{(T ) | T a template on a chain poset, valid with respect to G }.
T

Then we have

|(G , n)| = ecrit (nn/2)(1+o(1)).

Remark 9.1.11. Note that to compute the constant crit, we only need to evaluate (T ) for T ranging over the finite collection of valid templates on chain posets of length at most L = L(G ) as above, assigning each element a non-empty set.

As we will see later in Proposition 9.2.5, we can determine the exact maximum of (T ) for valid

templates T

on P([n]), and this value is crit

n n/2

(1 + o(1)). The lower bound in Theorem 9.1.10

will follow as in Remark 9.1.7.

Example 9.1.12.

· Suppose we are in the situation of Example 9.1.3 (1). The maximum crit is attained by the
template T on a length k - 1 chain poset, sending every element to the set {1}. Then crit = (T ) = log 2k-1, and |(G , n)| = 2(k-1)(nn/2)(1+o(1)), recovering the results of [105, 10, 45].

· Suppose we are in the situation of Example 9.1.3 (2). Then the following two templates T on chain posets realize crit. For x  y a chain poset of length 2, the first such template has T (x) = {3, 4}, T (y) = {1}, and the second has T (x) = {4} and T (y) = {1, 2}, attaining (T ) = crit = log 6. We thus have |(G , n)| = 6(nn/2)(1+o(1)).

The constant crit( ) is also very easy to compute analogously to crit, and yields the critical exponent in the following theorem.
Theorem 9.1.14. Suppose that G contains a monochromatic chain of every colour. With the probabilistic setup of Question 9.1.2, denoting p = (p1, . . . , pm) and V the number of validly coloured subsets (S, c) of (the coloured part of) P([n]) with respect to G , we have
E(V ) = ecrit (p)(nn/2)(1+o(1)).
As before, the methods from [64] analogously answer the dense cases where there is some colour without a monochromatic forbidden chain. Theorem 9.1.14 thus completes the analysis of Question 9.1.2 in the sparse case.
For the remainder of the chapter, we write coloured sets as S rather than (S, c), suppressing the colouring c.

9.2 Strategy and auxiliary results
In this section we outline the strategy of the proof and present some auxiliary results. We start by formulating a weighted version of Theorem 9.1.10 and Theorem 9.1.14 which encompasses both of them. Throughout the rest of the chapter we always assume that G contains a monochromatic chain of each colour and refer to such a G as sparse. In this section, we let G := G2  . . . 9.4 Crude upper bound
Assume G contains a monochromatic chain of every colour (of the m colours). Then there exists
L = L(G ) such that no validly coloured set (S, c) contains a chain of length L. We can easily show |(G , n)|  (m + 1)(L-1)(nn/2)(1+o(1)) using the L-chain containers constructed in [10].
Indeed, in [10] it was shown that there exists a family C of subsets of P(n) such that |C |  2o(1)L(nn/2), |C|  (1 + o(1))(L - 1) nn/2 for every C  C and every set S  P(n) that is L-chain free is contained in some set C  C . To conclude, note that there are at most (m + 1)(L-1+o(1))(nn/2) coloured subsets of C for each C  C giving a total of at most 2o(1)L(nn/2) × (m + 1)(1+o(1))(L-1)(nn/2) = (m + 1)(1+o(1))(L-1)(nn/2) coloured sets that are L-chain free.

Chapter 10
Strong Ramsey Games in Unbounded Time
The work in this section is joint with Stefan David and Ivailo Hartarsky and is published in the European Journal of Combinatorics [50].
10.1 Introduction
10.1.1 Background
Positional games were first studied by Hales and Jewett [88] and Erdos and Selfridge [62]. The general setting was given by Berge [22], but the field was shaped by the numerous works of Beck since the early 80s including [13-15, 17-20]. Though the most natural positional games are the strong ones, in which both players compete to achieve the same objective, the theory has largely deviated from this direction due to the prohibitive difficulty of strong games. Many weak variants have been developed and have proved more suitable for study, like Maker-Breaker games, for which much is known (see e.g., [19]). However, the strong games remain very poorly understood.
In the present chapter we study instances of a particular type of strong game - the strong Ramsey game. This was first introduced by Harary [89] for cliques and later for arbitrary graphs [90]. In the strong Ramsey game, or simply the Ramsey game, R(B, H) on a finite or infinite graph B, called the board, with target graph H, two players, P1 and P2, alternate to take previously unclaimed edges of B, starting with P1. The first player to claim an isomorphic copy of H wins and if none does so in finite time, the game is declared a draw. More generally, one might take B and H to be r-uniform hypergraphs. This setting was already mentioned by Beck and Csirmaz and Beck in [13, 20].
For fixed graphs B, H, in R(B, H), a very general strategy stealing argument due to Nash shows that P2 cannot have a winning strategy. Moreover, for any fixed target graph H it follows from Ramsey's theorem [126] that R(Kn, H) cannot end in a draw for n sufficiently large. Therefore, in R(Kn, H) P1 has a winning strategy for n sufficiently large. The strategy given by this argument is

250

Strong Ramsey Games in Unbounded Time

not explicit, and indeed almost no examples of explicit strategies are known. In particular, no explicit strategy has been exhibited for R(Kn, Kk) with k  5 and n large.
This fact makes it difficult to attack most natural questions in the field. For instance a notorious open question popularised by Beck [17-19] asks if, for fixed k, in the game R(Kn, Kk), P1 has a winning strategy in bounded time as n  . An easy compactness argument shows that this is equivalent to the game R(K , Kk) being a P1-win. The answer is conjectured to be in the affirmative [17, 18, 125] but no progress has been made on the problem for k  5. Beck emphasised the importance of the question when he listed the question among his "7 most humiliating open problems" [19]. Another notorious open question asks if for every fixed target graph H in the game R(Kn, H), P1 has a winning strategy in bounded time as n   or, equivalently, R(K , H) is a P1 win. In a recent paper [94], Hefetz, Kusch, Narins, Pokrovskiy, Requilé and Sarid addressed the natural generalisation to hypergraphs, and changed the intuition about this phenomenon completely. Surprisingly, in [94] they exhibited a target 5-uniform hypergraph H for which they constructed an explicit drawing strategy for P2 in the game R K(5), H . This result provides strong evidence that just strategy stealing and Ramsey-type arguments are insufficient to attack Beck's conjecture.
However, the corresponding question for graphs, as asked in [94], still remains open. As we explain in Section 10.1.3, as the rank decreases this question becomes much harder. In this chapter we make further progress in decreasing it. In our first result, Theorem 10.1.2 (see Corollary 10.1.3), we exhibit a graph G (in fact G = K6 \ K4, see Figure 1), for which we prove that in R(Kn  Kn, G) P1 does not have a winning strategy in bounded time. In order to do so we build on the work of [94]. However, there is a serious obstacle in adapting the strategy developed in [94] from 5-uniform hypergraphs to graphs. As we further explain in Section 10.1.3, although the strategy developed in [94] is a strong game drawing strategy, the core of the argument is a weak game fast winning strategy that is inapplicable to graphs. Therefore, most of our effort is spent developing a much more intricate and elaborate strategy for graphs reflecting characteristic difficulties of strong games, which we discuss further in Section 10.5.
Turning to hypergraphs, in our second result, Theorem 10.1.7 (see Corollary 10.1.8), we exhibit a 4-uniform hypergraph G, obtained by adding 2 new vertices and including them in all edges of G, for which we prove that in R Kn(4), G P1 does not have a winning strategy in bounded time. In order to do so we cover the board Kn(4) with copies of the board Kn-2 (2) , noting that Kn(4) = X=Y V (K(4)) Kn-2 X,Y ,
n
where Kn-2 X,Y is the set of hyperedges containing X and Y , naturally identified with Kn-2. While in the graph setting the strategy is considerably more involved, in the hypergraph setting the strategy is much more difficult to analyse.
It is not clear whether one should expect R(Kn, G) to admit a bounded time winning strategy for P1, but it is known (see [33] for a simple proof) that for having a winning strategy on Kn is equivalent to having one on Kn  Kn. Thus, by compactness, our result refutes one of two natural conjectures extending simple finite board facts to infinite boards. Namely, it is not the case that on the one hand

10.1 Introduction

251

for every graph H R(K , H) is a P1-win and on the other hand for every graph H if R(K , H) is a P1-win, then R(K  K , H) is also a P1-win.

In this section we state our results.
Definition 10.1.1. Let G = K6 \ K4 be the graph with edge set E(K6) \ E(K4) (see Figure 1). In the graph setting the central result is as follows.
Theorem 10.1.2. The game R(K  K , G) is a draw. As mentioned in the previous section, one can also formulate the result for finite boards. A
standard compactness argument gives the following result.
Corollary 10.1.3. In the game R(Kn  Kn, G) P1 does not have a strategy which guarantees him a win in a bounded number of moves, as n  .
Remark 10.1.4. Note that we obtain an explicit drawing strategy for the first player in R(K , G). An amusing corollary of Theorem 10.1.2 is the following.
Corollary 10.1.5. It is not the case that on the one hand for every graph H R(K , H) is a P1-win and on the other hand for every graph H if R(K , H) is a P1-win, then R(K  K , H) is also a P1-win.

Strong Ramsey Games in Unbounded Time

10.1.3 Overview
Graph setting Throughout the chapter, unless otherwise stated, all configurations are considered after P1's move and before P2's move. The graph G is formed by a base and four pairs of edges connected to it (see Figure 1). We decompose the graph G into a pair and a core which is formed by the base and the other three pairs.
We construct the drawing strategy for P2 in the game R(K  K , G) in three stages. We denote the copy of K in which P1 takes his first edge by K1 and the other copy by K2.
In the first stage P2 builds a core in K2. While doing this P2 ensures that the following two statements hold. On the one hand, during the entire stage P2 remains ahead of P1 in building G in K2, which is intuitively possible, since P2 is the first player in K2. In particular, at the end of the first stage P1 does not have a threat in K2. On the other hand, at the end of the stage P1 has at most |E(G)| - 1 edges in K1. At the beginning of the second stage P2 checks if P1 has a threat in K1. If this is the case, P2 blocks P1's (possibly infinite) threats as long as P1 keeps making new ones in K1. The graph G is such that P1 cannot force a win by making such consecutive threats. If at some point P1 does not have a threat in K1, then, in the third stage, P2 aims to build the pair in K2 and complete G. He does so by making a (possibly infinite) series of threats from a well-chosen endpoint of the base to new vertices. The choice is such that P1's responses to P2's threats cannot be part of a threat of P1.
Roughly speaking, in [94] the strategy is divided into the same three stages: building a core, blocking threats and then making threats. However, there is a severe obstruction in transferring the first stage of the strategy from 5-uniform hypergraphs to graphs. Indeed, in [94] the target hypergraph has a non-trivial and identifiable core that admits a weak game fast building strategy (with number of moves equal to the size of the core). The construction of such a core relies heavily on the high rank. Unfortunately, for graphs this is not feasible anymore because the construction of any non-trivial identifiable core can be delayed by P1. Therefore, in the graph setting one should abandon such weak game strategy and face the essential difficulty of strong game strategies, by allowing delay and making sure to block the other player while building one's own target graph.
Hypergraph setting As it was discussed in the introduction, the board K(4) can be viewed as X,Y V K(4) K  X,Y , where KX,Y = {e  E(K(4)), X ,Y  e} identifies naturally with the board K . Note
that a copy of G is contained in exactly one of the boards KX,Y and identifies with a graph G in this board.
The drawing strategy of P2 in the game R(K(4), G) still follows the same three stages. In the first stage P2 builds a core in a board KX,Y (corresponding to K2 from the graph setting) disjoint from P1's first hyperedge by using a very simplified version of the first stage of the strategy for R(K  K , G), which exploits the larger number of symmetries in higher rank. P2 ensures that at the end of the first stage P1 does not have a threat in any board KX,Y , except possibly exactly one of the six boards containing P1's first hyperedge. The second and third stages remain unchanged. However, in the hypergraph setting we need a more subtle analysis than in the graph setting, as the different boards

10.2 Preliminaries

253

intersect. The key observation is that for {X1,Y1}  {X2,Y2} =  the boards KX1,Y1 and KX2,Y2 share only one hyperedge. In this respect it is clear that higher rank simplifies the problem.

10.1.4 Organisation of the chapter
The rest of the chapter is organised as follows. In Section 10.2 we establish the key lemmas which are used repeatedly in the proof. In Section 10.3 we construct a drawing strategy for P2 in R(K  K , G). In Section 10.4 we derive a drawing strategy for P2 in R K(4), G . In Section 10.5 we make some concluding remarks and state some open questions.

10.2 Preliminaries
10.2.1 Setup and notation
For this section and the next one we consider the game R(K  K , G), where the graph G is defined below. Denote the two disjoint copies of K constituting the board by K1 and K2 and assume without loss of generality that P1's first edge is taken in K1.
For technical reasons, we consider the version of the game in which P1 stops playing after a finite number of moves, and the game continues until P2 builds a copy of G. In order to show that P2 has a drawing strategy, it is enough to show that at the end P1 does not have a copy of G.
Definition 10.2.1. Let G = (V, E) be the graph with vertex set V = {A0, A1, B1, B2, B3, B4} and edge set E = {A0A1, A0Bi, A1Bi | i = 1, . . . , 4} (see Figure 1). With a slight abuse of notation we shall refer to any isomorphic copy of G as G.
We call the edge A0A1 the base of G. Note that any automorphism of G fixes the base, so it is well defined.
For the following set of definitions we consider the game at a certain stage. Recall from Section 10.1.3 that all configurations are considered after P1's move and before P2's move.
Definition 10.2.2. We say that a given graph G is P1-free if P1 has no edges in G and we say it is P2-free if P2 has no edges in G.
Definition 10.2.3. For a given graph G, let eP1(G) be the number of edges in G taken by P1 if G is P2-free; otherwise, define it to be 0. Define similarly eP2(G). For a given vertex A let degP1(A) and degP2(A) be the number of edges that contain A taken by P1 and P2, respectively.
Note that in the course of the game for a given G the quantities eP1(G) and eP2(G) may decrease, becoming 0.
Definition 10.2.4. We say that a vertex F is P1-free if degP1(F) = 0, we say it is P2-free if degP2(F) = 0 and we say it is free if degP1(F) = degP2(F) = 0.

254

Strong Ramsey Games in Unbounded Time

Definition 10.2.5. We call an edge A0A1 taken by P2 in K2 a potential base if there exist two vertices B1, B2 in K2 such that P2 has the edges A0B1, A0B2, A1B1 and A1B2 and there exists a special vertex X  {A0, A1} such that:
(i) P1 does not have a triangle X, T1, T2;
(ii) P1 does not have a 4-cycle X,C1,C2,C3 with the edge XC2 not taken by P2.
Remark 10.2.6. If the edge A0A1 is a potential base with special vertex X, then if later in the game P1 constructs an additional star XF1, XF2, . . . from X to P1-free vertices F1, F2, . . . together with exactly r extra edges, then he has at most r triangles sharing a common edge that contain X.

10.2.2 Lemmas
In this section we present the main technical result, Lemma 10.2.7, which states that if the game has reached certain configurations, then P2 has a drawing strategy. This result is used recurrently in conjunction with the strategy constructed in Section 10.3. In order to check one of the hypotheses of Lemma 10.2.7 we also establish Lemma 10.2.11.
Lemma 10.2.7. Assume that before P2's turn, the game has the following properties:
(a) P1 has at most six edges in K1;
(b) for any G in K2, eP1(G)  5;
(c) P2 has a potential base A0A1.
Then, P2 has a drawing strategy.
Proof of Lemma 10.2.7. Let E 10 and E 20 be the set of initial edges of P1 in K1 and K2 respectively. Without loss of generality let A0 be the special vertex of the potential base A0A1. The line of play of P2 is divided in three stages as follows. In the first stage, P2 takes edges from A1 to free vertices Fi for i = 1, . . . , k until at some point P1 does not take the edge A0Fk (this necessarily happens, since P1 makes only a finite number of moves). Then P2 takes A0Fk and P1 plays somewhere. Let E 21 = {A0Fi, 1  i < k} be the star taken by P1 and E2 be the set consisting of the last two edges taken by P1 (if he did not stop playing).

Claim 10.2.8. One can guarantee that after a finite number of moves, called second stage, the configuration (before P2's move) satisfies the following two properties.
· For each G in K1 we have that eP1(G)  7. · The set of edges P1 has taken in K2 is E 20  E 21  E 2 for some E 2 with |E 2|  2.
